[001] USER
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
we have the following assesment at this stage of development, please review and answer if we should start over or continue given the analysis below: 
The opening is the boundary between an agent and its environment:

The agent keeps its native UI, model connection, subscription, and provider authentication.
Its working directory is an existing Stratura Tip.
Its native session identifier/state is associated with the existing Session where supported.
Shell, network, deployment, and other external effects go through the existing effectd/Plane path.
Handoff uses the existing lease and bond machinery.
Final authority is the Tip snapshot and diff—not whatever the agent claims it changed.

That is already consistent with Stratura’s locked substrate ADR, Tip/Plane/Session contract, and decision to preserve agents’ native interfaces.

The real support picture
Agent/tool	Native seam Stratura can use	Credible outcome
OpenCode	Plugins can intercept tools and override built-ins by name; headless OpenAPI/SSE server; resumable/forkable sessions	Best complete reference adapter. Replace bash, fetch, etc. with effectd implementations while preserving OpenCode’s UI and provider auth. Plugins, server
Codex	App-server has thread start/resume/fork, streaming events, remote environments, and an execution/filesystem/HTTP protocol	Very strong architectural fit: Codex stays the agent while Stratura supplies its execution environment. Some remote-environment surfaces are experimental, so certify exact Codex versions. App-server, exec protocol
Claude Code	Lifecycle hooks can block or modify tool calls; cross-machine SessionStore; managed sandboxing and custom network proxy; MCP	Mandatory market adapter and feasible, but hooks alone are insufficient. Full mediation requires managed settings that redirect/deny Bash, WebFetch, computer-use, and other bypass paths. Hooks, cross-machine sessions, sandboxing
oh-my-pi	In-process SDK, configurable SessionManager, restricted tool sets, fail-closed tool interception, built-in shadowing, RPC/ACP	Excellent open conformance target and stress test for rich agents, though not the first distribution wedge. SDK, extensions
Generic CLI / Orca-style host	PTY, cwd, SSH, worktrees, agent-specific observational hooks	Broad “bring any CLI” compatibility for durable work, but not evidence of complete effect mediation. Orca’s “any CLI” claim illustrates the reach—and the assurance limitation. Orca

So the first-party launch set should be:

Claude Code — proves relevance.
Codex — proves the remote-runtime architecture.
OpenCode — proves complete, open, inspectable mediation.
oh-my-pi — conformance/reference implementation.
Generic CLI — broad fallback, clearly described as workspace-compatible rather than fully mediated.
Three facts to report, not three new product objects

Do not label an agent simply “supported.” Report three independent capabilities:

Durable work: Can it operate correctly on a Tip and survive machine handoff?
Native continuation: Can its own conversation/session resume on another runtime?
Verified effects: Can every relevant non-file side effect be prevented from bypassing effectd?

For example, an arbitrary CLI can have durable work without native continuation or verified effects. Claude Code can have all three, but only under the managed integration. This prevents “works in a terminal” from being confused with “securely mediated.”

Architectural decisions worth locking now
Define the trusted host boundary.
The pinned agent binary and Stratura adapter are trusted host components. Model-generated commands and subprocesses are untrusted. The host agent may reach its model/auth endpoints; generated shell, fetch, deployment, and service actions must cross effectd.
Tighten the evidence claim.
Tip file writes are controlled by the writer lease and evidenced by snapshots/diffs. effectd evidences mediated external effects. Native model-provider traffic is agent-owned and is not deterministic/replayable. “Every tool effect” should not imply that model traffic or direct Tip writes are journaled as effectd calls.
Separate session state from credentials.
Claude, Codex, OpenCode, and OMP commonly key session state to cwd or place it near credential state. Give Tips stable workspace identity/path mapping, but never teleport an agent’s entire $HOME. Provider credentials remain device-local or explicitly brokered; they do not enter the Tip.
Handoff means restart/resume, not process migration.
Quiesce the agent, finish or cancel outstanding effects, flush native session state, seal the run, transfer the lease, remount, and resume on the destination. This matches ADR-0002’s existing decision that live process teleport is not the default.
Treat MCP and PTYs as mechanisms, not security boundaries.
MCP can expose effectd tools but does not disable native tools. A PTY can observe and steer an agent but cannot prove that the process did not bypass mediation.
The release bar

For every certified agent/version, automate this test:

Start the real unmodified agent UX against a Tip.
Edit files and run a realistic test/build workload.
Verify direct network and shell bypass attempts fail.
Transfer the Tip while proving the source is fenced read-only.
Resume the same agent session where its native API supports it.
Continue with a different agent using the same durable work.
Confirm no provider or cloud credentials entered the Tip.
Verify the external-effect chain and independently inspect the final diff.
Accept, reject, or integrate changes entirely through existing Stratura controls.

The flagship demonstration should start in Claude Code on a laptop, hand the Tip to Codex on cloud compute, and open the result in OpenCode elsewhere. Do not promise lossless Claude-to-Codex “brain transfer.” The universal contract is portable work, authority, and evidence; native conversation continuity is an adapter-specific enhancement.

That is the landing: Stratura moves the desk reliably, resumes the same agent when possible, and lets another agent take over without surrendering control.

Stratura should be the durable execution envelope and handoff control plane for agents people already use—not another agent runtime, chat application, or universal LLM gateway.

At the reviewed main commit, the repository has built an impressive perimeter around a missing center. It can create and clone desks, transfer writer authority, start an audit record, materialize tool artifacts, stream UI events, and render conflict demos. But it cannot yet take an ordinary existing agent and manage the complete lifecycle implied by your sentence:

Run it → preserve its work → stop or move it → restore what can be restored → safely rebind tools and credentials → continue → review → integrate or discard.

That is where the product currently falls through the seams.

The central missing object

You have three partial representations:

Desk/Tip: filesystem state and lineage.
Session: largely modeled as a REPL/chat event stream.
Run: largely an evidence and journal boundary.

None of them currently represents the actual user-owned workload across its lifecycle.

I would not introduce another public noun. I would expand Run so it joins:

desk tip
+ agent/runtime profile
+ command and working directory
+ executor placement
+ tool/service/model requirements
+ credential grants
+ policy and assurance level
+ native continuation handle, if any
+ lifecycle state
+ outputs, effects, and final disposition

Today, agentdesk run start creates an effectd audit run; it does not launch an arbitrary command. The Firecracker supervisor similarly boots a fixed proof workload rather than accepting an agent command, PTY, environment, state paths, or resume behavior. CLI, supervisor.

That is not merely missing UX. It is the missing product join.

Where you went astray

The original primitives were mostly right. In fact, ADR-0002 already says the right things: teleport is the wedge, files move, and processes restart. The native surface document also correctly says Stratura is not the agent’s chat UI.

The drift happened in the consequences and sequencing:

You optimized for an ideal future runtime instead of existing messy agents.

The architecture assumes agents will use Stratura’s capability ABI through effectd. Claude Code, Codex, OpenCode, and similar systems already own their shell, network, authentication, models, session formats, and UI. They will not naturally reorganize themselves around that ABI.

You equated moving the desk with continuing the work.

A lease transfer moves write authority. A mounted snapshot moves files. Neither tells Stratura what command was running, how to stop it, which state is portable, what must be rebound, or how to confirm that it resumed correctly.

“Tool plane” became artifact distribution rather than dependency resolution.

The current provider contract is primarily warm and materialize, with local/bonded/checkpoint grades. Real agent environments also depend on installed CLIs, MCP servers, SaaS APIs, local daemons, OAuth sessions, hardware, subscriptions, secret scopes, and sometimes model endpoints. Plane specification.

Chat leaked back into the product model.

The repository says Stratura is not the chat app, yet the unified surface stream centers message.delta, tool cards, and REPL behavior. Meanwhile basic workload lifecycle events are absent; even run.started is merely reserved for a future version. Session event schema.

You pursued surface symmetry instead of complementary surface roles.

Phones acquired local effectd, native toolkits, leases, offline queues, and broad host contracts. But the primary phone value is likely: observe, approve, stop, redirect, hand off, and review—not reproduce a Linux agent environment.

You built exceptional-path UX before the happy-path closure.

Conflict classes, inboxes, LLM merge proposals, and multi-platform conflict rendering are detailed, but the integration handler still creates demo conflicts when real filesystem integration is absent. Current integration implementation. For the initial coding-agent user, “final changes” should first mean a normal diff, tests, commit/branch/PR, accept, or discard.

Security became an all-or-nothing architecture.

The security design is one of the strongest parts, but it currently presumes semantic mediation that unmodified agents will bypass unless Stratura controls the process boundary. The repository also acknowledges remaining storage-fencing and signing-key limitations, so some public claims run ahead of enforcement. Security fast-follows.

You extracted a platform before discovering the smallest useful integration.

Seven-language SDKs, every-OS host chrome, push fanout, mobile-native runtimes, and several provider types appeared before one reliable run existing agent → move → continue → review → accept path. The abstractions may be elegant, but they were extracted from architectural aspirations rather than repeated customer integrations.

The whole-surface gap
Surface	What the product promise requires	What is missing or misdirected
Installation	Adopt an existing repo and agent in minutes	Current quick start requires multiple daemons, Postgres, migrations, credentials, and manual IDs
Existing-agent execution	Launch an arbitrary CLI with cwd, PTY, environment, status, stop, and output	Current Run is an audit record; supervisor is a fixed proof harness
Durable work	Files, artifacts, runtime-owned portable state, provenance, and a handoff note	Desk primarily contains work/, out/, tmp/, and metadata
Continuation	Honest distinction between filesystem continuation, native session resume, and process checkpoint	Current language sometimes collapses all three into “resume”
Handoff	Quiesce, fence, snapshot, resolve the destination, relaunch, verify, and roll back on failure	Current handoff primarily transfers a lease to a device
Dependencies	Detect and resolve executables, services, MCP, SaaS, local daemons, hardware, and optional model endpoints	Plane focuses on content-addressed artifacts and a closed capability ABI
Credentials	Scoped references and grants that can be rebound without copying ambient secrets	Strong platform auth exists, but not a practical credential broker for the launched agent
Effects	Useful safety for unmodified agents, with deeper mediation when integrated	Existing agents can execute outside the semantic effect ABI unless sandboxed at the process/network boundary
Final changes	Diff, tests, artifacts, external effects, accept/integrate/discard	Conflict UX is ahead of basic filesystem/Git closure
Collaboration	Hand work to a person, agent, pool, or machine—with acceptance and context	Handoff is device-centric rather than work-assignment-centric
Surfaces	Execute on compatible hosts; supervise and review everywhere	Too much pressure for every surface to implement equivalent local machinery
Developer platform	A small adapter discovered from working integrations	SDK breadth arrived before the adapter contract was proven
LLM routing in this model

You do not need a first-class LLM router.

You need a general binding resolver, and a model endpoint is one optional binding type:

executable
service / HTTP API
MCP server
local daemon
model endpoint
credential reference
hardware or platform capability

An agent that owns its model and authentication declares no model binding. A tool expecting an OpenAI-compatible endpoint can request one. A destination might resolve that to Ollama, LiteRT-LM, Apple’s local facility, vLLM, or a remote provider.

The important product behavior is not “route every LLM call.” It is:

Before relocating a run, determine whether the destination can satisfy its requirements, which credentials may be granted there, which substitutions are allowed, and what will degrade.

That generalizes to tools with no LLM involvement at all.

Continuity must become explicit and honest

Every move should report what continues:

Work continuation: files and artifacts move. This should be universal.
Agent-session continuation: native history/state resumes through a runtime-specific adapter.
Process continuation: memory/process checkpoint restores. Optional and probably rare.

For example, an adapter can identify the executable, separate portable session state from secret-bearing host state, provide a native resume command, and declare requirements. It should not standardize prompts, messages, model selection, or the agent’s tool loop.

The generic process runner comes first. Native adapters are incremental upgrades.

Security should become an assurance ladder

“Best-in-class enterprise security” still matters, but it should stop being a binary prerequisite:

Reversible: isolated COW work, clear diff, rollback/discard.
Contained: restricted writable paths, network policy, scoped secret injection.
Mediated: integrated semantic capabilities through effectd, approvals, richer policy.
Attested: signed evidence, stronger key custody, verified execution.

An unmodified agent can deliver value at the first two levels. Integrated runtimes can reach the latter levels. The UI and evidence must state the actual level rather than implying every effect was mediated.

This makes security an adoption accelerator and enterprise moat, rather than an integration tax that prevents the base product from being useful.

The golden path I would make the organizing principle

Conceptually:

stratura run -- claude
Adopt the current directory into an isolated desk tip.
Launch the existing agent unchanged.
Track its workload lifecycle and reversible changes.
When requested, quiesce and snapshot it.
On the destination, resolve runtime, service, model, and credential bindings.
Resume the native agent session when supported; otherwise relaunch honestly against the same work with a handoff note.
Show files changed, tests, artifacts, approvals, and external effects.
Integrate into Git, preserve as a branch, hand to someone else, or discard.

The corresponding product surfaces become straightforward:

Agent UI: Claude Code, Codex, OpenCode, Macro, etc.
CLI/desktop host: launch and execute.
Web/mobile: observe, approve, stop, redirect, hand off, and review.
Runner: remote compute.
Stratura: owns durability, placement, bindings, control, evidence, and closure.

No Stratura chat interface is required.

What I would freeze versus preserve

Preserve:

COW desks, tips, snapshots, leases, and fencing.
Runtime-independent process isolation.
Policy and effect journal.
Tool-plane negotiation, generalized into dependency bindings.
Native-host boundary.
Verification and provenance.
Conflict-safe parallel work.

Freeze until the golden path works:

llm.chat as a central product capability.
CRIU/process teleport as a headline.
Full local mobile tool planes.
Seven-language partner SDK expansion.
Every-platform chrome completeness.
Custom LLM conflict resolution.
More push, conformance, multi-region, or confidential-compute surface area.

Freeze does not mean delete. These are downstream capabilities that need a proven workload lifecycle to attach to.

The revised hypotheses

I would formally retire:

Resumable agent interactions anywhere.

Replace it with:

Durable, transferable agent work across existing runtimes.

And refine the security hypothesis to:

Every run is reversible and attributable by default, with progressively stronger containment, mediation, and attestation.

So the wedge becomes portability plus closure. Security remains the enterprise unlock and long-term moat.

The governing test for every part of main should now be:

Does this help an unmodified agent enter durable work, continue after a relocation or handoff, or let the owner safely accept or reject the outcome?

If it does none of those, it may be good infrastructure—but it is not part of the product’s critical path yet.

## Verdict: continue — do not start over. The assessment is accurate, and its own findings argue against a restart.

I verified the assessment's load-bearing claims against the code at `main` (commit `8924edf`) before answering. Every one of them checks out.

### What I verified

- **The center really is missing.** The only workload runner in the entire system is `agentdeskd run --desk … --prompt …`, which starts an effectd run record, makes exactly one `http.fetch` with `{"prompt": …}` to an env-configured URL (or just `clock.now` if unset), optionally spawns the supervisor, and seals the run (`agentdeskd/src/main.rs`, `run()`). `agentdesk run start` in the CLI only POSTs a run record to effectd — no command, cwd, PTY, or process (`cli/cmd/agentdesk/main.go`). The supervisor guest makes a single mediated capability call and powers off (`supervisor/guest/main.go`). There is no argv launch, no long-running process management, no quiesce/stop, no adoption of an existing directory — anywhere.
- **Session model is chat-shaped, lifecycle-shaped events absent.** `message.delta` is a first-class payload; `run.started` is literally a comment in a `reserved 19 to 29` block (`proto/agentdesk/v1/session_events.proto`).
- **Integration fabricates evidence.** When no real filesystem integration exists, the integrate handler manufactures a demo conflict with hashes `blake3:demo-left`/`blake3:demo-right` and a canned 0.55-confidence merge proposal (`controld/internal/httpapi/phase6_conflict.go`).
- **Security claims run ahead of enforcement**, exactly as stated: `docs/FAST_FOLLOWS.md` admits direct JuiceFS/TiKV access can bypass fencing and a compromised effectd host can re-sign forged chains.
- **Perimeter-before-center is real.** Seven SDK languages ship (`sdk/`), and the most striking artifact: `surfaces/ios/toolwrap/requirements.json` is a meticulous plan for running Claude Code *on iOS* — libnode in webcontent, a PTY worker for the Ink TUI, Safari OAuth loopback — while no PTY-based agent runner exists on Linux, where the daily loop actually lives. The hardest surface got the deepest agent-hosting design; the easiest surface got none.
- Minor hygiene: `plane/planectl` is an accidentally committed 9 MB macOS arm64 binary that should be deleted.

### Why continue beats restart

**The assessment's preserve list is the repository.** COW desks, lease CAS with generation fencing, the BLAKE3/Ed25519 audit chain with verify/replay, Cedar policy, the plane loader, the conformance suites — these are the hard-to-build parts, they work, they're tested, and the assessment explicitly says to keep all of them. A restart would spend its first phase rebuilding exactly this, then arrive back at the same golden-path gap with fewer assets.

**The missing center is additive, not structural.** Nothing in the architecture blocks a real workload lifecycle:

- `agentdeskd` already has mount, lease, fence, effectd client, and supervisor-spawn plumbing; a process runner (`argv`, cwd on the tip, env, PTY, capture, stop/quiesce) slots in beside them.
- The event schema *reserved tag space for run lifecycle events* — adding `run.started`/`run.stopped` is a planned-compatible extension, not a schema break.
- The trusted-host boundary the assessment proposes (pinned agent binary trusted, generated commands untrusted) is a relaxation of ADR-0002 §10's "agent loop outside the mediated guest tools," not a reversal. The mechanisms stay; what changes is which claims you make per run — the assurance ladder is mostly a documentation and reporting change, and editing claims is far cheaper than rebuilding daemons.

**The failure was sequencing, not primitives — and restarts don't fix sequencing.** The same team and process that built seven SDKs before one process runner would, in a rewrite, face the same temptations with a blank canvas. The corrective is the governing test the assessment proposes, applied to a codebase that already contains the substrate. Notably, the repo was already self-correcting in this direction: `docs/NATIVE_SURFACE_CHROME.md` (dated three days after ADR-0002) already declares "Agent Desk is a host, not an agent chat app; Claude Code is the agent UI." The docs pivoted; the code hasn't caught up. That is a continuation problem, not a foundation problem.

**What would justify a restart — and doesn't apply.** You'd start over if the locked substrate couldn't support the new center (it can), or if the security model had to invert (it doesn't; it has to be *graded honestly*). Neither holds.

### Where I'd amend the assessment before adopting it

1. **"Expand Run" needs one explicit decision.** Today `run_` is an effectd journal boundary; a user workload will span multiple effectd runs (relaunch after handoff at minimum — `docs/CRIU_SUPPORT.md` already says "new `run_id` per session"). Keep effectd runs as immutable evidence segments and put the workload lifecycle object in controld, referencing N evidence runs. Whatever the public noun, don't overload the sealed journal boundary with a mutable state machine. This is the first ADR to write.
2. **Shrink milestone 1 below the flagship demo.** Claude → Codex → OpenCode requires three adapters plus binding resolution plus credential brokering — that's a roadmap. Milestone 1 should be one agent, one loop: adopt cwd into a tip, launch unchanged, quiesce, transfer laptop → cloud, relaunch with a handoff note, show diff, accept/discard. The five-adapter "launch set" is exactly the breadth-first pattern that produced the current perimeter; guard against it.
3. **Name the adoption problem.** `stratura run -- claude` in an existing repo inverts the current model (desks live in JuiceFS; the user's repo lives on disk). Adopt-by-copy into a fresh tip is an honest v1; overlay/COW adoption of a local directory is new substrate work deserving its own ADR, not an assumed detail.
4. **Delete the demo-conflict fabrication now, not at freeze time.** A product whose thesis is untrusted-agent *evidence* must never synthesize fake conflicts with fake hashes on a real code path. Returning "no conflicts, here's the diff" is strictly better.

### Concrete next moves

1. ADR-0007: workload lifecycle noun and state machine (created → launching → running → quiescing → sealed → relaunched), owner controld, evidence = linked effectd runs.
2. Process runner in `agentdeskd`: launch arbitrary argv on the tip mount with PTY/env/capture/stop; emit lifecycle events in the reserved 19–29 tag space.
3. Single-command UX (`stratura run -- <agent>`) with adopt-by-copy, diff view, accept-as-commit/branch, discard; replace the demo-conflict path with real diff closure.
4. Reframe README and security docs to the assurance ladder (Reversible / Contained / Mediated / Attested) and report the actual level per run, plus the three facts (durable work / native continuation / verified effects) per agent.
5. First adapter: Claude Code under managed settings (hooks + denied bypass paths) at "Mediated"; generic CLI ships at "Reversible/Contained," labeled as such.
6. Apply the freeze list as written; also remove the committed `plane/planectl` binary.

The one-line summary: the assessment correctly identifies an impressive perimeter around a missing center — but the perimeter's foundation is precisely what you'd have to rebuild after a restart, and the center fits into it additively. Continue, redirect, and hold every new change to the governing test: does it help an unmodified agent enter durable work, survive relocation, or let the owner accept or reject the outcome.

[079] ASSISTANT

Elaboration below, grounded in the code. The short version: adoption is the one operation the substrate has never defined, it is now the first thing every user experiences, and the two obvious implementations differ enormously in how much of the locked architecture they disturb — which is why it needs to be a named, decided thing rather than an implementation detail of the golden path.

## 1. Why it's an inversion: a desk's birth story

Every desk today is born *inside* the fabric. The control plane's full route table offers exactly two origins: `POST /v1/desks` (a registry row plus an empty skeleton from a base template) and `POST /v1/desks/{id}/clone` (a `juicefs clone` of an existing desk) — see `controld/internal/httpapi/router.go`. There is no import/adopt/ingest verb in the API, the protos, or the CLI. Content currently enters a desk only through the file gateway or scripted seeding (`scripts/seed-demo-desk.sh`).

Every guarantee then hangs off the assumption that the work lives under the fabric's mount root: desk paths are derived as `<mount_root>/desks/<desk_id>` (`agentdeskd/src/mount.rs`), lease fencing is implemented as remount-read-only *of that path* (`agentdeskd/src/fence.rs`), the agent cwd is `<desk>/work` (`agentdeskd/src/supervisor.rs`), snapshots describe JuiceFS state, teleport is a remount, and prefetch warms fabric blocks. None of that machinery can see, fence, snapshot, or move a directory sitting in `/home/user/myrepo`.

`stratura run -- claude` starts from precisely that directory. So the golden path's step one — "adopt the current directory into an isolated desk tip" — is the single operation for which the substrate has no primitive. That's the inversion: today state flows fabric → mount → user; adoption requires user's disk → fabric, with the guarantees attaching mid-flight.

## 2. Why it's now the product-defining moment, not a detail

Two reasons this deserves a name and an ADR rather than an unprincipled `rsync` in the CLI:

**It becomes the headline SLO.** Every SLO in ADR-0002 §7.3 measures moving desks that already exist in the fabric (warm attach ≤ 300 ms, handoff ≤ 5 s). Under the golden path, the first sixty seconds of every new user's life is adoption latency: time from `stratura run` in an existing repo to the agent's first keystroke. If adopting a typical repo means ten minutes of uploading, the wedge dies in the demo. This SLO doesn't exist yet because the operation doesn't exist yet.

**It's a consent and residency moment.** ADR-0002 §5.4 makes the object store the source of truth, and the deployed configuration uploads blocks to R2/Azure. Adoption therefore means the user's proprietary code starts leaving their machine the moment they run the first command. For the same CISO who is the audience of the security story ("don't let it torch prod / leak secrets," ADR-0002 §1.2), silent first-command exfiltration to a third-party bucket is a liability. Whether adoption defaults to local-staged-until-handoff, prompts for consent, or requires tenant-owned buckets is a decision with enterprise consequences — not something to discover in code review.

## 3. Adopt-by-copy: honest, but with six real decisions inside it

The v1 shape: create a fresh desk tip, copy the working tree into `work/`, record provenance, launch the agent with cwd inside the mount. Its virtue is that *after the copy completes, the desk is an ordinary desk* — fencing, snapshots, diffs, teleport, and the audit bindings all mean exactly what they claim, with zero new substrate semantics. The copy is the price of keeping the evidence claims true.

But "just copy" hides six decisions that should be made deliberately:

1. **Git handling.** Copying `.git` wholesale drags in hooks (executable code), remote URLs that sometimes embed tokens, and potentially huge pack history. Skipping it breaks the agent (agents run `git status` constantly) and breaks closure. The right cut is a git-aware adoption: record the base commit and remote, materialize a clone plus the dirty diff inside the desk, exclude derived directories (`node_modules`, build caches) by default and let the tool plane re-materialize toolchains — which is what the plane is for. This bounds copy cost to history + dirty files instead of the full tree.
2. **Secrets policy.** Real repos contain `.env`, `.npmrc`, `.aws` credentials. The assessment's own release bar says "confirm no provider or cloud credentials entered the Tip" — without a default exclusion/scrub list and an explicit warning at adoption time, the very first adoption violates the flagship test.
3. **Path identity.** Adopted work lands at a desk-id-derived path, not `/home/user/myrepo`. Claude Code and peers key native session state to the cwd, so the path change silently severs "native continuation" — the agent sees a brand-new project. If native resume is a product claim, the adoption contract must include a stable, reproducible per-project workspace path (an alias mount recreated identically on the handoff destination). That's a contract decision, not a UX nicety.
4. **Divergence and write-back.** The original repo keeps living while the agent works in the desk; the user may edit it. So "accept" must be a git operation — emit a branch/commit onto the recorded base in the original repo and let git own any merge — never a file-level overwrite of the user's tree. And never adopt in place (bind-mount over or mutate the original): reversibility is the first rung of the assurance ladder, and adoption-in-place forfeits it at the exact moment a new user trusts you least.
5. **Consent/residency default**, per §2 above.
6. **Provenance.** Record source device, absolute path, git remote + HEAD, dirty-file digest, and the applied adoption policy in `.desk/meta.json`. This is what makes "final authority is the Tip snapshot and diff" meaningful for adopted work — the Tip's *origin* is attested, and the handoff note has a factual basis.

There's also one substrate interaction even in the copy path: ADR-0002 §7.2 deliberately pins `--writeback false` for durability and fencing reasons, but adoption wants the copy to complete at local-disk speed with asynchronous upload. A phase-dependent writeback policy (writeback during adoption, mandatory flush barrier before snapshot seal or handoff) touches the fencing guarantees — exactly the kind of cross-cutting nuance the ADR format exists to settle.

## 4. Why overlay/COW adoption is new substrate work

The seductive alternative: zero-copy adoption — make the desk a copy-on-write overlay whose lower layer *is* the user's local directory. Instant start, no storage until writes. It feels like "just overlayfs." Four reasons it is not:

1. **Lower-layer mutability.** Overlayfs requires an unmodified lower layer; users will keep editing their repo underneath. The outcomes are undefined behavior or silent corruption — in a product whose entire thesis is trustworthy evidence. Avoiding it requires filesystem-level snapshots (btrfs/ZFS/LVM/APFS — each platform- and filesystem-specific) or a watch-and-invalidate protocol. Either is a subsystem.
2. **Teleport breaks.** The lower layer lives on one machine's disk, so an overlay-adopted desk cannot move until it hydrates into the fabric, and the source machine must stay online throughout. That introduces a desk lifecycle state — *partially hydrated* — that leases, snapshots, prefetch, the SLO table, and the "R2 is the source of truth" rule (§5.4) do not currently represent. Every one of those contracts needs an amendment to stay honest.
3. **Snapshot and evidence semantics.** `snap_id` currently describes fabric state. With an external lower layer, a snapshot is sound only if it also captures that layer — by hashing the local tree (expensive and racy against concurrent edits) or pinning to a git commit plus dirty overlay (covers only tracked files). If the lower layer can drift mid-run, "this run was bound to snapshot X" stops being true, and the audit chain inherits a hole it cannot detect. That's an integrity regression dressed up as a performance optimization.
4. **The platform matrix.** Overlayfs is Linux-only. The golden-path laptop is disproportionately macOS; Windows is harder still. Day-one overlay adoption means building three platform-specific mechanisms or shipping a Linux-only wedge.

Note what those four points have in common: each one amends a *locked* ADR-0002 decision (desk layout §5.2, snapshot definition, SLO table §7.3, writeback §7.2, object-store source of truth §5.4). In this repo's own discipline, "changes the darts already thrown" is the definition of needing a new ADR. Treating it as an assumed detail of the golden path is how it would end up half-built inside the CLI with the integrity hole shipped silently.

## 5. The staged path I'd write into the ADR

- **v1 — adopt-by-copy, git-aware.** Copy bounded to history + dirty files; derived directories excluded; plane re-materializes toolchains; provenance recorded; accept = branch on the recorded base. All existing guarantees hold unmodified. Implementable with pieces that already exist (mount, file gateway, `.desk/meta.json`).
- **v1.5 — background hydration.** Launch the agent against locally staged desk state while upload proceeds; define the flush barrier that gates snapshot seal and handoff. A small, bounded relaxation with one new rule.
- **v2 — lazy/overlay adoption**, only if measured adoption latency on large real repos actually breaks the SLO — decided by data, not anticipation. This is where the platform-specific COW work lives, if it's ever justified.

The acceptance test for the ADR mirrors the golden path itself: `stratura run -- claude` in a real multi-GiB repo reaches first agent keystroke within the adoption SLO; the Tip contains no credentials from the source tree; handoff to a second machine reproduces the workspace path; accept lands a clean branch on the original repo's recorded base; discard leaves the original untouched. If those five hold, adoption is solved — and only the first one ever justifies revisiting overlay adoption.

3. Adopt-by-copy: honest, but with six real decisions inside it
The v1 shape: create a fresh desk tip, copy the working tree into work/, record provenance, launch the agent with cwd inside the mount. Its virtue is that after the copy completes, the desk is an ordinary desk — fencing, snapshots, diffs, teleport, and the audit bindings all mean exactly what they claim, with zero new substrate semantics. The copy is the price of keeping the evidence claims true.

[129] ASSISTANT

[132] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Search complete — 21 queries across GitHub, arXiv/ACM, vendor docs, and standards bodies, Jan 2025–Aug 2026. The sweep broke two of my assumptions outright, revised four, confirmed three, and surfaced a competitive shift that changes where the wedge should sit. Findings below, mapped to the specific claims in §3 and §4.

## Assumptions that broke

**1. "The copy is the price of keeping the evidence claims true" — false dichotomy.** I framed the choice as copy (slow, sound) vs. overlay mount (instant, unsound). There is a third primitive I ignored, and in 2025–26 it became available on all three platforms: file-level copy-on-write clones. APFS `clonefile(2)`/`copyfile(3)` gives O(1) directory-tree clones on the macOS default filesystem ([kernel details](https://www.macinternals.app/en/blog/apfs-clones-and-snapshots)); Linux has `FICLONE`/`copy_file_range` reflinks on XFS/btrfs/bcachefs and now [OpenZFS block cloning](https://openzfs.github.io/openzfs-docs/Basic%20Concepts/Data%20Storage/Block%20Cloning.html); Windows 11 24H2/Server 2025 made ReFS block cloning automatic in plain `CopyFile` on [Dev Drive](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/dev-drive/) ([CoW-in-Win32](https://devblogs.microsoft.com/engineering-at-microsoft/copy-on-write-performance-and-debugging/)). A clone-copy is instant like an overlay but sound like a copy: after the clone, both sides own independent extents, so the user editing their repo cannot corrupt the desk — the lower-layer-mutability objection simply doesn't apply. Two caveats keep part of my claim alive: reflinks cannot cross filesystems, so this requires a local staging tip on the same volume with asynchronous fabric ingestion (which promotes my "v1.5 background hydration" to the actual v1), and defaults vary (APFS: everyone; ext4/NTFS: fallback to real copy).

**2. The platform matrix inverted.** I argued "the golden-path laptop is disproportionately macOS" as a reason overlay adoption fails. With clonefile, macOS is now the *easiest* adoption platform, not the hardest. And for the exec side, Apple open-sourced [Containerization](https://github.com/apple/Containerization) and the [`container` CLI](https://github.com/apple/container) (WWDC25, 48k stars), with WWDC26 adding persistent "container machines" — sub-second lightweight Linux VMs native to Apple silicon. The repo's `vz_container` provider direction is validated; a Contained/Mediated rung on Mac laptops no longer depends on Docker Desktop.

## Assumptions that need revision

**3. Overlay adoption isn't hypothetical — it shipped, holes included.** Turso's [AgentFS](https://turso.tech/blog/agentfs) (beta) is literally §4's design: `agentfs run bash` overlays your directory as a read-only base with a SQLite delta layer (FUSE on Linux, NFS on macOS), whiteouts, per-operation audit, snapshot/branch, cloud sync, and a [disaggregated-on-S3 roadmap](https://penberg.org/blog/disaggregated-agentfs.html) with lazy-loaded migration. Docker Sandboxes' `--clone` mode mounts the repo read-only and has the agent work on a private clone inside a microVM. So the demand and mechanism are proven — but my soundness objections survive contact: AgentFS reads pass through to the live base, so user edits mid-run still drift under the snapshot, and teleport still requires materializing the base elsewhere. Nobody shipped base-pinning. The ADR should cite these as prior art with a known integrity hole Stratura can close (pin the base to a git tree + clone the dirty set), not as evidence the problem is easy.

**4. "Hashing is expensive, snapshots of live trees are racy" — mostly obsolete.** The research frontier treats layered-COW freeze as the snapshot: [DeltaBox](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22781) (May 2026) checkpoints agent sandboxes in ~11 ms by freezing the writable layer and rolls back in ~2 ms, explicitly built on XFS+reflink. [Jujutsu](https://github.com/jj-vcs/jj) makes snapshotting a VCS operation (working-copy-as-commit, op log, stale-workspace detection that mirrors lease-fencing semantics), and people already use it as [agent checkpointing infrastructure](https://www.panozzaj.com/blog/2025/11/22/avoid-losing-work-with-jujutsu-jj-for-ai-coding-agents/). Hugging Face's [Xet protocol](https://huggingface.co/docs/xet/en/index) (open spec + Rust crates, Git-LFS-compatible transfer agent) makes CDC dedup at 8–128 KiB chunks with BLAKE3 Merkle hashes a solved ingestion problem — directly usable for making adoption upload cheap and dedup-friendly.

**5. Git handling: the industry converged on worktrees, not clone+diff.** Claude Code ships [`--worktree` and `isolation: worktree` for sub-agents](https://agentpatterns.ai/workflows/worktree-isolation/), Codex uses background worktrees, and an entire tool category ([Conductor](https://www.conductor.build/docs/concepts/git-worktrees), Sculptor, Crystal, AWF) manages per-agent worktrees with branch-native closure. This upgrades my §3 recommendation: adoption should be *worktree-shaped* — a fabric-resident object store with tips as worktrees — so the agent's work is a branch of the user's repo from birth and write-back collapses into `git fetch`/merge. The known limitation (worktrees don't isolate runtime state) is precisely what desks + effectd add.

**6. Path identity: still real, but three standard resolutions appeared.** The [Agent Client Protocol stabilized `session/resume`](https://agentclientprotocol.com/announcements/session-resume-stabilized) (April 2026; a *required* baseline method in ACP v2) with explicit cwd per resume — an agent-agnostic continuation handle of `(agent, sessionId, cwd)` instead of per-agent hacks. Claude Code's own [session teleport](https://proflead.dev/posts/claude-code-session-teleportation/) keys continuation to *repo + pushed branch*, not path. Codex sessions are portable JSONL files with [`codex resume --all` across directories and `thread/fork` on the app-server](https://codex.danielvaughan.com/2026/04/13/codex-cli-session-persistence-resume-fork-analytics/). Stable path mapping remains a decision (ACP requires cwd match), but the adapter contract I said had to be "discovered" is being written by the ecosystem — consume ACP, don't invent.

**7. Consent/residency: the regulatory timeline I implied was wrong.** The Digital Omnibus (in force 27 July 2026) [deferred EU AI Act high-risk obligations](https://www.legalithm.com/en/blog/pren-18229-1-ai-act-logging-standard-what-it-says): Article 12 record-keeping now applies from 2 Dec 2027 (Annex III standalone) and 2 Aug 2028 (embedded). So compliance is a 2027–28 wave to be ready for, not an August 2026 forcing function — and the draft standard (prEN 18229-1, using ISO/IEC 24970's logging model) plus the six-month retention floor in Art. 26(6) give the evidence chain a concrete schema to map to when it lands.

## Assumptions that held

- **Never adopt in place.** Docker's own security docs flag their default direct read-write mount as having "no isolation between the agent and your workspace," including agent-config attack surfaces like `.claude/settings.json` ([Docker docs](https://docs.docker.com/ai/sandboxes/security/isolation/)). Reversible-by-default is validated as the differentiating stance.
- **Provenance needs attestation, and now has standard formats.** [gittuf generates SLSA Source Track attestations and VSAs](https://github.com/gittuf/gittuf/pull/1091) (experimental, late 2025); [PROV-AGENT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.02866) extends W3C PROV with MCP concepts; [OTel GenAI semantic conventions](https://github.com/open-telemetry/semantic-conventions-genai) (own repo since May 2026, still Development status) define agent/tool spans. The `.desk/meta.json` decision becomes "emit in-toto/SLSA-source-shaped attestations, export journal as OTel GenAI spans" rather than a bespoke schema.
- **External effects remain the unsolved gap — and the moat.** DeltaBox states plainly it has "no support for rollback of external side-effects such as network or database operations." Claude Code checkpoints [don't capture bash-driven file changes](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/checkpointing). Everyone solved file/process rollback in 2025–26; nobody solved external-effect accounting. That is effectd's territory, verbatim.

## New territory the search surfaced

**The isolation layer commoditized — the wedge must move up-stack.** In the survey window, `sbx run claude` shipped ([Docker Sandboxes](https://www.docker.com/blog/docker-sandboxes-run-claude-code-and-other-coding-agents-unsupervised-but-safely/): per-agent microVMs on all three OSes, network allow/deny, credential proxy, `--clone`), GitHub made a default-on, org-managed, [audited agent firewall](https://github.blog/changelog/2026-04-03-organization-firewall-settings-for-copilot-cloud-agent/) normal for all four major engines ([AWF](https://github.github.com/gh-aw/reference/network/) logs every domain decision), Anthropic open-sourced [sandbox-runtime](https://github.com/anthropic-experimental/sandbox-runtime) (Seatbelt/bubblewrap/WFP + deny-by-default proxies, ~295k weekly downloads), and Kubernetes grew a [Sandbox CRD](https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/agent-sandbox) with warm pools and pause/resume. "Run your agent safely in an isolated workspace" — the front half of the golden path — is now table stakes. What none of them have is exactly the back half Stratura built: durable cross-machine work objects, single-writer authority, tamper-evident signed evidence, cross-agent handoff, and diff-first closure. Three beachheads follow: (a) srt's proxy hop is a natural interposition point — point it at the effectd gateway and unmodified agents get journaled, policy-gated egress without cooperation, a partial Mediated rung I previously thought required managed settings; (b) the K8s `Sandbox` CRD has no workspace/teleport/evidence story — a Stratura runner behind that API is a clean enterprise-platform entry; (c) [AgentSight's eBPF boundary tracing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.02736) (TLS-level intent + kernel-level effects, <3% overhead, zero instrumentation) enables an "Observed" assurance rung between Contained and Mediated on Linux runners.

**Vendor session portability validates the loop and squeezes single-agent versions of it.** Claude Code now has cloud sessions, one-way `--teleport` to local (requires a clean checkout of the same repo and a pushed branch), and `/remote-control` to phone; Codex has named/pinned/forkable threads. The vendors are each building a single-agent, vendor-cloud version of Stratura's daily loop — but their transfer medium is a pushed git branch plus a clean tree, which is precisely what a Stratura tip provides on any machine. Interoperation, not competition: the tip carries the branch; `claude --teleport` and ACP `session/resume` provide native continuation on top.

**Process continuation got cheap, changing grade F.** [Morph Infinibranch](https://cloud.morph.so/docs/developers) branches running Firecracker VMs in <250 ms and markets it specifically for agent exploration; DeltaBox does ms-level combined fs+process checkpoint. Since the repo already runs Firecracker, grade F via VM snapshots (rather than the CRIU hooks in ADR-0003) is now the low-cost path on Linux runners.

## What this does to the ADR I proposed

The staged path revises to: **v1 = clone-copy adoption** (reflink/clonefile where the filesystem allows, real copy as fallback), worktree-shaped, into a local staging tip on the repo's own volume, with asynchronous CDC-deduped fabric ingestion and a flush barrier gating snapshot seal and handoff. Containment via srt/sbx-style wrapping with the proxy pointed at effectd; continuation via ACP `session/resume` as the primary adapter surface with `claude --teleport`/`codex resume` as native enhancements; provenance as SLSA-source/in-toto attestations; evidence export mapped to OTel GenAI conventions when they stabilize, and to prEN 18229-1 for the 2027 compliance wave. Overlay-mount adoption of a live directory stays deferred — now with the added argument that the one shipped implementation (AgentFS) carries the exact integrity hole §4 predicted. The single sentence that changes most: the copy tax I made §3's central price is largely gone on modern filesystems, so the honest v1 and the instant v1 are the same design — and the defensible product is no longer "adopt and isolate" (commoditized in the survey window) but "adopt, move, hand off, and prove," which is the part of the stack the 2025–26 wave conspicuously did not build.

Direct answer first: the largest pain today is not running agents — it's **verifying and landing what they did**. The beachhead is the **evidence-sealed run that lands as a reviewable unit** (branch + diff + attested account of everything the agent executed, fetched, and touched). The moat is being the **neutral, user-owned system of record for machine-made work, plus the authority primitives (leases/fencing) that coordinate it** — a position no agent vendor can structurally occupy. Here's the case, with the demand-side evidence.

## Where the pain is actually largest (mid-2026, measured)

**1. The verification/landing bottleneck — largest and best-evidenced.** The 2026 DORA AI-assisted development report and related datasets show the constraint has fully relocated: teams merge [98% more PRs while time-in-review jumped 441%, and 31% of PRs now merge with zero human review](https://coommit.com/blog/ai-code-review-bottleneck-2026). Sonar's 2026 survey: AI writes 42% of committed code, [96% of developers don't fully trust it, and only 48% always verify before committing](https://dev.to/code-board/the-review-bottleneck-why-more-ai-code-means-slower-teams-in-2026-1e5n). Lightrun 2026: 43% of AI-generated changes need production debugging *after passing QA*; zero surveyed engineering leaders were "very confident." The two failure modes practitioners name are [rubber-stamping ("verification debt with a green checkmark") and queue collapse](https://realitygraph.dev/ai-code-review-bottleneck). And the emerging best practice is stated almost verbatim as Stratura's thesis: ["evidence attached to every change — the reviewer reads what was verified instead of re-deriving it."](https://realitygraph.dev/ai-code-review-bottleneck) Today that "evidence" is hand-assembled and unattested; nothing makes it trustworthy. One honest boundary: the *semantic* half of review (architecture, business logic) is not Stratura's to solve — the play is making the *forensic* half free and attested so scarce senior attention goes where only it can.

**2. Fleet sprawl across vendor silos — acute and unowned.** Running 2–5 parallel agents is now normal practice; the coordination tooling is embarrassingly ad hoc — [JSON registries in `~/.claude/worktree-registry`, hand-assigned ports, pre-merge overlap scanners](https://github.com/SnowWarri0r/parallel-worktree-dev), and the lived pain of [five terminal windows each silently blocked on a missed approval](https://codeongrass.com/blog/parallel-coding-agents-worktree-isolation-ownership/). Meanwhile every vendor ships its own parallelism (Claude subagents, Codex parallel agents, Copilot `/fleet`) and its own cloud, so work fragments across silos with no unified authority, status, or handoff. Note what's already commoditized inside this pain: file isolation (worktrees) and runtime isolation (containers/sbx). The unowned part is exactly Stratura's nouns — who may write where (authority), what state is it in (status), how does it move (handoff), in what order does it land (integration).

**3. The enterprise trust/audit gap — structural, slower-burning.** 31% zero-review merges is an incident postmortem waiting to happen; GitHub shipping [org-managed agent firewalls](https://github.blog/changelog/2026-04-03-organization-firewall-settings-for-copilot-cloud-agent/) proves the control demand; the EU logging wave lands Dec 2027–Aug 2028. This is real but it's the moat's direction, not the beachhead — compliance sales cycles are too slow to be the wedge.

## The beachhead

**Ship the sealed run artifact: every agent run lands as a branch + diff + signed forensic account — commands executed, network egress, packages installed, tests run — with one-click accept/discard.** Concretely: `stratura run -- claude` wraps commodity parts (clone-copy adoption on a worktree-shaped tip, srt/sbx-style containment with the proxy journaled through effectd) and adds the one thing nobody ships: the attested account, sealed against a workspace snapshot, verifiable without trusting the agent or the wrapper.

Why this wedge and not another:

- **It's single-player.** A tech lead adopts it alone by requiring "agent PRs come with a sealed run report." No team migration, no new workflow — it rides the worktree/parallel-agent behavior that already exists.
- **It attacks the measured pain.** The review-sandwich pattern teams already use (machine pass before human pass) is missing its trustworthy machine pass for *what the agent did* — AI reviewers read the diff; nobody attests the run. This is complementary to CodeRabbit-style semantic reviewers, not competitive.
- **It's small.** The commoditized layers (isolation, adoption mechanics, session resume via ACP) are consumed off the shelf, which answers your "less code to manage" instinct regardless of the restart decision. The differentiating code is the part this repo already has working: journal, hash-chained audit, snapshot/diff, seal.
- **It has a distribution hook.** The artifact is visible in every PR as a check plus report — the CI-badge dynamic; it spreads by being seen.
- **Everything the 2025–26 wave shipped conspicuously lacks it.** Docker sbx isolates but doesn't attest; GitHub's AWF logs domains but only inside GitHub's runner and only for GitHub's silo; Claude checkpoints [can't even see bash-driven changes](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/checkpointing); DeltaBox explicitly punts on external side effects. The gap is not contested; it's unoccupied.

The expansion path from that wedge is natural: sealed runs → a fleet plane (unified status, approvals, authority, handoff across agents and machines — the five-terminal pain) → compliance export mapped to prEN 18229-1/OTel GenAI when the 2027–28 wave arrives → enterprise runner behind the Kubernetes `Sandbox` CRD, which has isolation and lifecycle but no workspace, evidence, or authority story.

## The eventual moat

Three layers, in order of how they compound:

**1. The neutral evidence corpus.** A vendor-neutral, user-owned, tamper-evident history of machine-made work, accumulated per organization and bound to workspace snapshots. The neutrality is structural, not just positioning: Anthropic's logs cannot audit OpenAI's agent, GitHub's firewall audits only GitHub's runner, and each vendor's telemetry vouches for itself. Only a third party that all runs pass through can hold the unified record — and once an org has years of attested run history ("who, what, and why for every change a machine ever landed"), the switching cost is the institutional memory itself. This is the position CI systems and artifact registries occupied: boring, neutral, and in the write path.

**2. Authority primitives across heterogeneous surfaces.** Single-writer leases with provable fencing across laptop, cloud, and phone is hard systems work — already built in this repo, and absent from everything I surveyed (the state of the art elsewhere is an advisory JSON registry in a dotfile). As fleets grow, "who may write where, provably" becomes the coordination substrate, and whoever owns that noun owns scheduling, handoff, and placement later.

**3. The merge-gate position.** Sitting at accept/reject for agent work is the natural attachment point for policy, compliance export, and eventually routing — the layer that decides what lands can enforce everything upstream of it.

Equally important, what is *not* the moat and shouldn't be chased: isolation tech (commoditized by sbx/srt/K8s in eighteen months), session resume (vendor-owned and now ACP-standardized), model routing (crowded), and agent chat UX (the vendors'). The main competitive risk is GitHub copying the artifact for its own silo — the defense is exactly the neutrality plus local-first ownership that GitHub's business model prevents it from offering.

One closing observation that ties this to the shelved restart question without reopening it: the beachhead as defined needs roughly the parts of the existing codebase that work today (effectd's journal/audit/seal, snapshots and diff, lease fencing, the CLI) plus commodity third-party layers for everything else — so it is the same destination whether you strip down `main` or rebuild lean around those pieces. The strategy is invariant to that choice, which is a good property for a strategy to have.

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
All three corrections accepted, and they converge cleanly: the wedge is pain #2, with #1's evidence demoted from product to substrate and #3 demoted from wedge to compounding moat. What sharpens the picture is *why* Claude's attempt at #2 is bad — I pulled its internals, and the failure is structural, not polish. That structure is the beachhead definition.

## Why "agents talk to each other" fails structurally

A [reverse-engineering of Claude Code Agent Teams](https://dev.to/nwyin/reverse-engineering-claude-code-agent-teams-architecture-and-protocol-o49) shows the whole design: flat JSON files in `~/.claude/tasks/` and `~/.claude/teams/`, task claiming via `.lock` files, messaging via append-and-poll inbox files, **shared working directory**, and — their own table says it — **merge strategy: none, agents edit files directly**. The [official docs](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-teams) concede teammates can overwrite each other without careful file partitioning, that `/resume` doesn't restore teammates (the lead then messages ghosts), and there's an [open bug where the runtime silently falls back to isolated subagents](https://github.com/anthropics/claude-code/issues/34693) with no error.

Read as systems design, three things are missing, and they're the same three things missing from the worktree-registry hacks and every launcher dashboard:

1. **No authority layer.** Coordination is advisory (lock files, politeness) over shared mutable state. Nothing *enforces* who may write where. The known result from [Microsoft's five-agent experiment and the multi-agent overkill data](https://www.augmentcode.com/guides/when-multi-agent-ai-is-overkill): coordination-by-conversation compounds errors and its overhead eats the parallelism gains; contract-first partitioning is what actually works — and partitioning is only real if it's enforced.
2. **No durable lifecycle.** Runs aren't objects anything owns — restart the lead and the team's existence evaporates. Stall detection is "the user notices."
3. **Single-vendor by construction.** Claude teammates coordinate Claude teammates. Real fleets are Claude + Codex + Copilot + whatever's best per task, and each vendor's incentive is to make *its* orchestrator the top layer, which is exactly why none of them will coordinate rivals well.

The framing that falls out: the vendors are building **IPC** — messaging between agents. Nobody has built the **kernel** — allocation, isolation, authority, scheduling, lifecycle — that IPC presumes. Processes don't negotiate file locks by chatting; the OS enforces them. That kernel stance is the product.

## The beachhead, restated precisely

**The enforced coordination plane for a fleet of heterogeneous agent runs on one project.** Five primitives, all enforced rather than advisory, all coordinating *runs from outside* rather than reaching inside any agent:

1. **Allocation** — every run gets its own tip (worktree-shaped, clone-copy). A shared cwd is impossible by construction, which single-handedly deletes Claude Teams' worst failure mode.
2. **Authority** — a lease per tip with CAS generations and fencing. Double-writers aren't discouraged; they're fenced. This is the repo's crown-jewel machinery reapplied to worktrees instead of JuiceFS mounts.
3. **Lifecycle** — runs are durable objects (`running / blocked-on-approval / stalled / quiesced / landed`) that survive restarts of any terminal, lead, or laptop. No ghosts.
4. **Attention routing** — one inbox across all agents and machines: approvals, blockers, completions, ordered by what needs you now. This kills the five-terminals-one-missed-approval problem, which practitioners describe as the acute daily pain. Terminal-first (`stratura ps` + inbox); phone approve/stop as the fast-follow — validated by both Grass's pitch and Claude's own `/remote-control`.
5. **Landing** — an ordered merge queue per project, where accept shows the diff *plus the sealed forensic account*. This is where pain #1's asset lives now: not sold as review tooling against CodeRabbit's category, but present as the trust mechanism at the accept moment. It doesn't compete with semantic reviewers; it feeds them.

Cross-vendor from day one is cheap because the coordination is external: Claude, Codex, Copilot CLI, and OpenCode are just processes launched into allocated tips, with ACP `session/resume` and native resume/teleport used where they exist.

The buyer logic now also lines up with your third correction: this is sold as **throughput recovery** — run five agents without babysitting, collisions, or landing chaos — which is a productivity purchase, the one category that leads buys today. The evidence corpus and audit posture accumulate silently as exhaust, becoming the moat (neutral ledger of machine work, authority primitives, merge-gate position) without ever having to lead a sales conversation.

## What this does to the asset map — an honest reversal

Under this wedge, my earlier freeze list partially inverts. Pieces I told you to deprioritize move onto the critical path: the lease/fencing machinery, the conflict inbox, session events, presence/status chrome, and the approval-interrupt surfaces — those *are* the coordination plane. And the piece I treated as the untouchable core — the JuiceFS/TiKV/R2 desk fabric — moves *off* the v1 critical path: worktrees plus local clone-copy tips are sufficient enforcement substrate for a single-machine-or-few fleet, and the fabric re-enters only when cross-machine handoff joins the loop. That maps directly onto your restart instinct: the lean rebuild-or-strip target is the lease + lifecycle + inbox + journal/seal slice with commodity isolation underneath — a few thousand lines of genuinely differentiated code sitting on off-the-shelf everything else, whichever codebase lineage it ships from.

[157] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Found it — ["Federation over Text: Insight Sharing for Multi-Agent Reasoning"](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16778) (Yao, Rabbani, Zaheer, Li — April 2026, with a [reference implementation](https://github.com/dixiyao/FoT)). The direct answer: **adopt it — but as a third plane, not as the abstraction for the kernel.** FoT is a knowledge-federation design, and it slots beautifully on top of the coordination plane; it cannot *be* the coordination plane, and the paper itself is built on an assumption that makes this clear.

## What FoT actually federates

FoT's loop: each agent works on its own tasks independently, distills its reasoning traces into compact textual insights, ships them to a central aggregator, which curates a cross-task insight library that gets injected into future runs — no gradients, no shared weights, works across heterogeneous models. Measured results: ~24–25% accuracy gains and ~28% fewer reasoning tokens on their benchmarks.

Note the load-bearing premise: agents "do local thinking and self-improvement on their specific tasks **independently**." FoT federates *insights* precisely because it does **not** federate *state*. There is no shared mutable workspace anywhere in its setting. So using it to abstract the fleet kernel would be a category error — it's not a weaker answer to the coordination problem, it's an answer to a different problem, one that becomes available *only after* the kernel guarantees the independence FoT presumes.

## The two-plane distinction

The Claude Agent Teams autopsy from the last turn is exactly the boundary line:

- **Text is the right medium for the knowledge plane.** Knowledge is advisory, idempotent, loss-tolerant, and improves under aggregation. Staleness degrades it gracefully. Heterogeneous agents consume it natively — text is the one ABI every vendor's agent already speaks. This is where "abstract it over text" is exactly right, and where Stratura already gestures (the handoff note).
- **Text is the wrong medium for the control plane.** Authority must be enforced, exactly-once, atomic, and adversarially robust. A lease decided by an LLM reading prose is not a lease; it's Claude Teams' `.lock` files and mailbox JSON — coordination-by-politeness over a shared working directory, which is the failure you called bad. Allocation, fencing, lifecycle, and landing order stay CAS-and-fencing, not conversation. (When cross-org *structured* agent interop eventually matters, the wire for that is [A2A 1.0](https://github.com/a2aproject/A2A/blob/main/docs/announcing-1.0.md) with its signed agent cards and task lifecycle — also not prose.)

## What adopting FoT concretely adds: the memory plane

Here's the ingredient worth taking, and Stratura is unusually well-positioned to take it because the raw material already exists as evidence exhaust:

- **Source**: every sealed run already captures the full trajectory — journal, diffs, transcript references. That's precisely the "local reasoning trace" FoT wants, and today it's captured for audit and then never used again.
- **Distill at seal time**: a cheap offline pass extracts per-run lessons — build quirks, flaky tests, dead ends, "the migration script must run before the type-check," failure modes that recurred across the fleet.
- **Aggregate per project**: a curated, versioned insight library — FoT's central server, scoped to the org. Never cross-tenant (same caveat as chunk dedup: shared derived artifacts leak).
- **Inject at allocation**: materialize the library into every new tip in the shape agents already consume — AGENTS.md / Agent Skills files — so Claude, Codex, and Copilot all pick it up with zero adapter work. Skills living in the tip means the knowledge teleports with the work automatically.
- **The differentiator over naive FoT: attested insights.** A shared insight library is a shared prompt surface, which makes it a poisoning vector — the aggregator becomes a supply chain for prompts. Stratura can sign every insight with provenance back to the sealed runs that produced it, and gate library admission through the same inbox that gates merges. Nobody else can offer that, because nobody else holds the verified run history the insights distill from.

This upgrades the moat in a way I undersold earlier: the evidence corpus stops being a passive compliance record and becomes an *appreciating* asset — the fleet measurably improves as the ledger grows (FoT's numbers are the ROI pitch: fewer tokens, higher task success). Switching cost is no longer just "you lose your audit history"; it's "your agents get worse."

So the layering ends up as three planes with clean separations: **state** (tips, snapshots — enforced), **control** (leases, lifecycle, landing — enforced), **knowledge** (distilled insights — federated over text, FoT-style, signed). The kernel guarantees the independence; the evidence chain supplies trustworthy traces; FoT turns the traces into compounding collective skill. Each plane uses the medium that matches its failure tolerance — which is, I'd argue, the principled version of the instinct behind your question.

i would typically agree with this: "the JuiceFS/TiKV/R2 desk fabric — moves off the v1 critical path: worktrees plus local clone-copy tips are sufficient enforcement substrate for a single-machine-or-few fleet, and the fabric re-enters only when cross-machine handoff joins the loop." but even that microsoft experiment you quoted -- each of those agents IS effectively "cross-machine", but how you are defining handoff might be the decider here

[159] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------


[160] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
You've put your finger on exactly the right joint, and you're correct that the definition of handoff is the decider. Let me make the distinction precise, because it resolves the apparent contradiction — and it also turns out the "fabric off the critical path" claim survives, but for a better reason than the one I gave.

## Two things called "handoff"

Look at what actually crosses a machine boundary in the Microsoft-style swarm — or in today's realistic fleet of two local worktree sessions, a Codex cloud task, and a Copilot run in Actions. The fleet is thoroughly cross-machine as a *substrate* fact: five agents, five machines, three trust domains. But no *workspace* ever crosses a machine boundary mid-life. Each run's working tree is born on one substrate (materialized from a base ref), lives there, and dies there. What crosses machines is ref-shaped, not mount-shaped: the base at birth, the result at landing, and occasionally the baton in between. That's the distinction:

**Handoff-as-reassignment**: quiesce the run, seal it, publish the tip as a ref (branch + dirty state + session file + handoff note), transfer the lease, and the destination *materializes a fresh tip* from that ref and resumes or relaunches. The transport is git objects or any content-addressed store. This is a baton pass at rest.

**Handoff-as-migration**: the same live mutable tree follows you across machines — warm attach in 300 ms, mid-session, multi-GiB dirty set paged in on demand, stale writers physically remounted read-only. This is what the JuiceFS/TiKV/R2 fabric provides and what ADR-0002's demo was built around.

The Microsoft swarm — and every vendor-cloud agent run today — is 100% reassignment-shaped. Distribution at birth, reintegration at landing, zero migration. So "each agent is effectively cross-machine" is true and yet consumes no fabric, because the fabric solves migration, and fleets do reassignment.

## Why gate-fencing isn't a compromise — it's the only design that covers the topology

The deeper point your observation surfaces: in a reassignment world, the enforcement primitives must be *logical*, not *physical*, because the fleet spans trust domains you will never control. You cannot mount your distributed filesystem into Codex cloud, Claude's web VM, or GitHub's Actions runner. A control plane whose authority depends on owning the storage under every run caps you at substrates you operate. A control plane whose authority is a lease generation presented at the landing gate spans anything that can push a branch.

And gate-fencing is *sufficient* for integrity, because reassignment changes what the scarce resource is. With separate materialized copies, two runs physically cannot corrupt each other's trees — the shared mutable resources are only (a) the tip lineage (who may land what, in what order, onto which base) and (b) the assignment (which run owns which task). Both are control-plane rows, enforced with exactly the CAS-generation lease machinery the repo already has. A fenced run in someone else's cloud can still waste tokens — but it cannot land, and its staleness is recorded. Meanwhile ADR-0002's handoff contract survives almost verbatim: quiesce → seal → transfer lease → resume. Only one word changes — "remount" becomes "materialize from ref." The crown-jewel authority design relocates from the disk to the gate; nothing is discarded.

This also suggests authority itself gets the ladder treatment, mirroring the effects ladder: **advisory** (registries, lock files — the Claude Teams level), **gate-fenced** (lease presented at landing; stale generations refused — v1, works across all substrates), **storage-fenced** (physical write revocation — an upgrade available only on substrates you control: your laptop, your K8s Sandbox pool, a regulated runner). Same shape as Reversible/Contained/Mediated/Attested, and the ledger records which level each run actually had — which also handles the evidence variance honestly (a Copilot-in-Actions run lands with GitHub's firewall log and a diff; a wrapped local run lands with a full sealed journal; both are admissible, at labeled assurance).

## What actually calls the fabric back

Migration re-enters per trigger, not as a prerequisite:

- **Follow-me sessions**: mid-run movement of a large dirty set at interactive latency (the original laptop→cloud→laptop demo), where push/materialize round-trips break the SLO.
- **Materialization cost**: monorepos where clone + checkout + dependency warm-up dominates run startup, so tips-from-scratch stop scaling — though note the 2025–26 toolbox attacks this without a global POSIX mount (lazy-pull snapshotters, warm-pool snapshots, partial clone, CDC chunk stores).
- **Physical fencing as a requirement**: environments where a revoked writer must be provably unable to write anywhere, not merely unable to land.
- **Live observation**: a phone viewing a tip mid-run without the runner's cooperation.

And when a trigger fires, the thing that re-enters should probably be a *lighter* fabric than the one in the repo: content-addressed warm materialization (the Xet/Nydus shape — chunks plus lazy attach) rather than one globally mounted filesystem with TiKV underneath. JuiceFS/TiKV/R2 is one implementation of migration, not the definition of it.

So the refined claim, absorbing your correction: the fleet is cross-machine from day one, and the v1 kernel must be cross-machine from day one — in its *authority and landing* design. What stays off the critical path is not "cross-machine support" but the *migration transport*. Reassignment over refs, gate-fenced by leases, covers the actual topology of 2026 fleets — including every substrate you'll never be allowed to mount a filesystem into.

Good question to pin down precisely — the answer depends on which layer of celld you mean. Let me look at what celld/celld-fabric actually is before answering.

[164] ASSISTANT

[165] TOOL

[166] ASSISTANT
