[167] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Short answer: yes — but at the opposite layer from the one ADR-0002 rejected. What's back is celld's *coordination model*, not its *execution engine*. The distinction matters enough to spell out, because your ADR actually rejected the right half and the half that's returning is the half you didn't need then.

## What celld is, reduced to its two ideas

Looking at [celld](https://github.com/denoland/celld) (and your `celld-fabric` fork — "self-hosted globally identifiable distributed durable objects"), it's two separable things:

1. **An execution engine**: V8 isolates running Workers/Durable-Objects code on your machines.
2. **A coordination protocol**: every object is a named, single-writer, serialized-transition durable entity; **ownership is a lease record in an S3 bucket claimed by one atomic compare-and-swap** — no membership protocol, no failure detector, no consensus service; state replicates to the bucket as the sole source of truth; cells have alarms, hibernate when idle, and hand off gracefully when a node drains.

ADR-0002 rejected celld as a *runtime foundation* — meaning idea 1: agent workloads were not going to run inside V8 cells. Everything since in this conversation has reinforced that rejection: the agents are foreign native processes (Claude Code, Codex, Copilot) you coordinate from outside, on substrates you don't control. They will never live in an isolate you host.

But look at idea 2 next to the kernel we converged on:

| celld | Fleet kernel |
|---|---|
| Cell ownership = bucket record claimed by CAS | Lease = row claimed by CAS with generations |
| Exactly one owner per cell, enforced | Exactly one writer per tip, enforced |
| Single-threaded cell, serialized transitions | Run/tip/lease as objects with serialized state transitions |
| Bucket as sole coordinator, no consensus service | Gate-fencing against a shared authority store, spanning substrates you don't operate |
| Alarms | Lease TTL sweeps, stall detection, approval timeouts |
| Hibernation | Idle runs costing nothing |
| Graceful cell handoff between nodes | Reassignment handoff |
| Evidence on disk: ownership records, SQLite, logs | Inspectable ledger and journal |

That's not resemblance; it's isomorphism. "CAS-and-fencing, not conversation" *is* the Durable Objects thesis — one writer per named object, storage transitions that never interleave, coordination through claimed ownership rather than messages hoping to be honored. The Claude Agent Teams autopsy was, in celld's vocabulary, "they built mailboxes without cells."

## So what does "back" mean in practice

Three layers, three different answers:

**As the discipline — yes, unconditionally.** The kernel's control-plane objects (run, tip lineage, lease, inbox item, insight-library entry) are Durable-Object-shaped whether or not any celld code is involved. The repo already implements the core of it independently: lease CAS with generation fencing in controld/Postgres. Your instinct when you forked celld-fabric was right about the primitives; the original ADR's mistake wasn't rejecting celld, it was that at the time the product was imagined as "run agents on our substrate," which made celld's *compute* the relevant half. Now that the product inverted to "coordinate foreign agents from outside," the relevant half inverted with it.

**As a protocol to steal — yes, and it solves last turn's problem specifically.** The bucket-CAS ownership scheme is a concrete answer to "how does authority span trust domains you can't deploy into": any runner that can perform a conditional write against object storage can claim, renew, verify, or discover a lease — no controld endpoint exposed, no daemon installed in Codex cloud or an Actions runner. That is gate-fencing with the gate itself decentralized to a bucket you own. The precedent for how to adopt it is already written in ADR-0002's own celld clause: *vendor the algorithm, not the runtime* — the same move it made with the Wasmo mediation code. Take `protocol.rs`'s shape (ownership records, CAS claims, lease discovery), not the daemon.

**As the runtime for the kernel — maybe later, with a clear test.** Running controld's objects *as* cells (celld, or your fork) would buy per-object serialization without lock discipline, alarms for free, hibernation, and bucket-only ops with no database to run. It costs a V8/JS dependency under your authority layer and a bet on a young runtime whose own README says the surface is still evolving. The authority layer should be the most boring thing you own, so Postgres CAS wins today. The decision test for every future component: **does it hold authority state with serialized transitions? Then it's cell-shaped, and celld semantics apply. Does it execute foreign agent code? Then it's a process or VM, and celld stays rejected.** A live per-project inbox cell fanning out WebSockets is cell-shaped; an agent run never is.

One corroborating data point that the shape is right: the search surfaced [crewd](https://github.com/dionanos/mcp-crewd-rs), an independent "cell fabric daemon for AI agents" — spawn/coordinate Claude and Codex worker *cells* over a Unix socket, SQLite WAL store, hash-chained audit log, session continuity by session/thread id. Someone else derived cells-for-agent-fleets from first principles, single-machine scope, mailbox-first. It validates the noun while leaving the cross-machine, authority-enforced, landing-gated version — your version — unbuilt.

but from this discussion, isnt this just: "check in your agents transcript/session files and use worktrees" ? doesnt that close out the entire gap you just articulated?

[169] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------


[170] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Mostly honest answer: that reduction is real, it's the floor, and conceding it precisely is the best way to see what's left — because what's left is exactly the product, and it's the same thing that was left after "just use git" in 2008.

## What the reduction genuinely covers

"Worktrees + check in the session files" covers the **state plane** almost completely. Allocation: each run gets a worktree. Distribution and reintegration: branches, push, fetch — content-addressed, cross-machine, works on substrates you don't control. Weak continuation: Codex sessions are JSONL files on disk; Claude's own teleport is already branch-mediated ("same repo, pushed branch"). Even the knowledge plane's *distribution* half reduces to check-in — that's why insight injection should be repo files (skills/AGENTS.md-shaped) in the first place.

And the proof that this reduction works is that the entire ecosystem already ships it: every worktree manager, Claude's `--teleport`, Codex's session files, Agent Teams' task JSONs. Which is also the proof of what it doesn't do — because the measured 2026 pains are the pains of teams *already using* the reduction: 441% review latency and 31% zero-review merges, five terminals blocked on unseen approvals, teammates overwriting each other, transcripts nobody can trust. The gap I articulated isn't an alternative to the convention; it's what remains after the convention is universally adopted.

## What survives the reduction, tested one primitive at a time

**Authority — does not reduce.** Git records what happened; it has no opinion about who *may* do what, now. Nothing in worktrees-plus-branches prevents two runs claiming one task, nothing revokes a run's authority mid-flight, nothing makes a stale writer's landing *refusable by generation*. Worktree locks are single-machine advisory; branch protection is a server rule you'd have to program with identities, leases, and generations — and the moment you write those rules, you are building the gate. The reduction doesn't eliminate the control plane; it smuggles it in as "just add some CI checks," which is like saying a mutex is "just a convention about a file."

**Lifecycle and attention — does not reduce, at all.** A branch cannot tell you the run producing it is stalled, looping since 3 a.m., or silently blocked on an approval you didn't see. Git is an at-rest medium by design; the acute daily pain (the five-terminals problem) is a *live-state* problem. Durable run objects, stall timers, and one cross-agent inbox have no representation in refs. This is the piece the beachhead leads with, and the reduction touches none of it.

**Evidence — actively worse under the reduction.** A checked-in transcript is *self-reported by the process under suspicion* — the exact thing the README's opening line warns about ("trusting logs the agent itself could have written"). Claude's own docs concede its checkpoints can't see what bash subprocesses did. An attested account requires an observer outside the agent — journaled proxy, snapshot diff, eBPF — none of which is a git convention. Plus practical holes: transcripts contain secrets and env material (checking them into repo history is a leak vector), vendor-cloud sessions live server-side where you can't check in what you don't possess, and a checked-in Claude transcript does nothing for a Codex resume — cross-agent continuation still rides the tip and handoff note, which the reduction merely relocates, not replaces.

**Coordination state — wrong datastore.** Leases, assignments, approval queues, and landing order are live mutable state with serialized transitions — the celld-shaped objects from the last turn. Git has no CAS on shared state across concurrent untrusted writers unless a server enforces it, and "the git server's hooks enforce it" is, again, just the control plane wearing git hosting as a costume.

## The frame that settles it

Git already was the "just check it in" reduction — for code, twenty years ago. Everything that became the actual industry sat *on top of* that at-rest core: identity, branch protection, review, CI, audit, merge queues. Nobody says GitHub is "just check in your code," even though, at rest, it literally is. The fleet kernel stands in the same relation to agent runs that GitHub/CI/branch-protection stands to commits: the live, enforced, attested layer wrapped around a content-addressed at-rest substrate — and *of course* the substrate is git; that was last turn's conclusion about reassignment-over-refs.

One calibration concession, because it's true: for one developer with two agents, the reduction is genuinely sufficient, the same way one person with one script never needed Jenkins. The kernel starts paying at fleet scale — N runs across M substrates with approvals, ordering, and someone accountable for what lands. So the crisp version of the answer to "doesn't this close the gap?": it closes the half that was already commoditized, defines the interface for the half that isn't, and the half that isn't — enforced authority, live lifecycle and attention, and an account of the work that doesn't depend on the worker's honesty — is the product.

[171] USER
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
how do you auto-enforce authority at this resolution. what you seem to be describing isnt "r/w/x" authoirty or basic capability grants but codebae specific authority

[172] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------


[173] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
This is the right question to press on, because the answer determines whether "enforced, not advisory" is real or marketing. You're correct: this isn't r/w/x, and it isn't classic capabilities either. POSIX-style authority protects *objects at rest* — files, sockets. Codebase authority is over four things that don't exist at rest: the **namespace** (paths-as-modules), **contracts** (interfaces, schemas), **invariants** (tests), and **history** (what may land onto what, in what order). None of those is visible to a filesystem. The reason it's still mechanically enforceable is an architectural consequence of everything we've established: isolation makes write-time enforcement unnecessary, which relocates authority to the two moments the kernel fully owns.

## The relocation: don't police writes, construct worlds and gate joins

Inside its own tip, an agent writing anywhere is harmless — that's what isolation-by-construction bought. So authority doesn't need to intercept writes in real time (the thing that made fine-grained enforcement seem impossible). It attaches at:

**Moment 1 — materialization: the workspace *is* the capability.** The grant determines what world the run is born into. A task scoped to `src/auth/**` gets a tip materialized with that subtree writable and everything else absent or read-only — plain `git sparse-checkout` in cone mode plus read-only binds does this today, no exotic machinery. The agent physically cannot wander outside its grant because the unauthorized world doesn't exist in its tip. This is the same design philosophy as ADR-0002's no-egress guest: no rule to evaluate, no bypass to find — the world is constructed so only authorized objects are reachable. Effects (exec, network) stay on the existing capability path (Cedar/effectd/proxy).

**Moment 2 — landing: the gate runs decidable checks over the diff.** Landing is a serialization point you own — a CAS on the project's history — and at that point every "codebase-specific" rule becomes a mechanical predicate:

- **Footprint ⊆ scope**: set inclusion of changed paths against the grant's fileset. Trivially decidable.
- **Contract preservation**: the grant says "implementation yes, public surface no" — enforced by the breaking-change tooling that already exists per ecosystem: `buf breaking` for protobuf, `cargo-semver-checks`, API-extractor surface diffs, OpenAPI diffing, migration linters for schemas. The codebase declares its contracts as machine-checkable artifacts; grants reference them; the gate runs the checkers.
- **Pinned invariants**: named test suites that must pass *and may not be modified in the same landing*. This one predicate kills the best-known agent failure mode — editing the test to make it pass. Diff-touches-pinned-file is set membership; suite-passes is a build result.
- **Authority freshness**: the lease generation presented with the landing must be current — the CAS-and-fencing core, unchanged, now applied per scope rather than per tip.

## What the grant object actually is

A run's ticket becomes: `{tip lineage, path scope (fileset), contract set (surfaces it may not break), pinned invariants (suites it must pass, may not touch), effect capabilities, landing preconditions (base freshness, ordering), escalation routes}`. That's a capability — but over namespace, contracts, and history rather than inodes. And scopes compose upward into scheduling: the allocator refuses overlapping scope claims within a lineage epoch, which is the database intention-lock idea applied to the repo tree — tips were coarse locks; scopes are fine-grained leases on the namespace, granted by the same CAS machinery.

None of this is speculative — each piece already runs at industrial scale, just aimed at humans instead of agent runs: Bazel's `visibility` rules are mechanically enforced module-level authority in every large monorepo; CODEOWNERS plus branch protection plus merge queues are landing-time enforcement; [gittuf](https://github.com/gittuf/gittuf) enforces *who may write which ref and path* via signed in-repo policy with its own authorization chain — which also answers the recursive question of who may widen a scope: grants are themselves files whose changes pass through the same gate under a stricter grant.

## The honest boundary — and why it doesn't break the design

There is an undecidable remainder, and it's important not to pretend otherwise: "this change alters the data model in a way that matters three services down" is not a predicate. The 441%-review literature says exactly this — the scarce thing is architectural judgment. The kernel's answer is that the *undecidable* part gets mechanical **routing**, not mechanical judgment: footprint patterns map to required approvers (the CODEOWNERS move), the gate enforces that the routing happened, and an LLM classifier may pre-sort — as advisory evidence, never as the lock. Enforce what's decidable; route what isn't; record both.

Two failure modes need explicit handling. Shared-by-necessity files (lockfiles, generated code, formatters) break clean scope partitions — they need declared merge classes with union/regenerate semantics, which is, notably, exactly what the repo's `CONFLICT_MERGE` path-class design already sketches; it was built for the wrong layer (conflict UX) but it's the right primitive for scope classes. And out-of-scope work shouldn't be discarded — the gate severs the diff: in-scope lands, out-of-scope becomes a *proposed task* carrying its own requested grant, routed through the inbox. Enforcement that converts violations into structured proposals rather than wasted tokens is what makes agents-under-authority economically tolerable.

So the resolution ladder, fully stated: **effects** (capability-gated, exists), **workspace** (one writer per tip, by construction), **namespace** (scoped materialization + footprint checks), **contracts** (breaking-change tooling), **invariants** (pinned suites), **history** (generation-fenced ordered landing), **semantics** (routed, not decided). Everything above the last rung is auto-enforceable with boring, existing machinery — and the last rung is where the humans you're trying to un-bottleneck are supposed to spend their attention anyway.

ok im starting to lose the product clarity here -- what are we even discussion as a concrete product or shippable demo?

[175] ASSISTANT

[176] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Fair — let me collapse it all back down. Everything we've converged on is one product with four commands.

## The product, one sentence

**A fleet console and landing gate for coding agents:** run any mix of Claude/Codex/Copilot/whatever in parallel, each in an isolated scoped workspace; one inbox shows what needs you; nothing lands without a fenced, ordered, *attested* merge.

## The product, concretely: four commands

```
stratura run --scope 'src/auth/**' -- claude "fix token refresh"
stratura ps
stratura inbox
stratura land
```

1. **`run`** — allocates a tip (worktree + clone-copy, sparse-checkout to the scope), launches the unmodified agent inside it, optionally wrapped in srt/sbx containment with egress journaled through the mediator. Every run is a durable object with a lease, not a terminal tab.
2. **`ps`** — the fleet on one screen: which runs are working, stalled, blocked on approval, finished. Survives terminal death; covers local runs first, registered cloud runs later.
3. **`inbox`** — every approval, blocker, stall, and pending landing across all agents, in one queue, answerable from there. This kills the five-terminals-babysitting problem, which is the acute daily pain.
4. **`land`** — the gate: footprint ⊆ scope, pinned tests untouched and passing, contract checks, current lease generation, ordered merge. Out-of-scope work is severed into a proposal instead of discarded. Every landing carries the **sealed run report**: the diff plus a hash-chained, signed account of every command, network call, and install — evidence generated by infrastructure, not self-reported by the agent.

That's v1 entire. Target user: the person who already runs 3+ agents in terminals today. Single-player adoption, no team migration.

## The shippable demo (10 minutes, one real repo)

1. Launch three agents on three scoped tasks — `claude` on auth, `codex` on billing, another `claude` on docs. Three `run` invocations.
2. `stratura ps` — one screen replaces three terminals.
3. One agent hits a permission prompt → it appears in `inbox`, approve it there without hunting for the right window.
4. Kill a terminal mid-run → `ps` still shows the run; it resumes. (Runs are objects, not tabs.)
5. The billing agent strays into `src/auth/**` → at `land`, the gate refuses that slice mechanically, lands the in-scope diff, and files the stray as a proposal.
6. One agent "fixes" a failing test by editing it → refused: pinned invariant.
7. Land the rest in order; open one sealed report: diff, every command, every host touched, tests run — then `verify` the chain and show a tampered row failing.

Closing line of the demo: *"Five terminals became one inbox, and nothing landed without a fenced, signed account of what actually happened."*

## What v1 is explicitly not

Not a chat UI, not an isolation runtime (srt/sbx/worktrees are consumed, not rebuilt), not session sync (vendors own resume; we just record the handles), not the JuiceFS fabric (reassignment-over-refs suffices until a migration trigger fires), not compliance export, not the insight library. Those are phase 2+: cloud-run registration and phone approvals next, then the FoT-style insight plane and fabric-on-trigger.

## Build reality

Four components: the process runner (new, small), the run ledger + lease/scope machinery (exists — controld's CAS/fencing, re-aimed at worktrees), the gate (new, mostly glue over git + existing checkers like `buf breaking`/pinned suites), and the seal/verify path (exists — effectd's journal and audit chain). Roughly: two existing crown jewels, two thin new pieces, commodity everything else — which is the same answer whether you strip `main` down or restart lean.

**Go new — a lean rebuild that transplants the two crown jewels as libraries. Don't fix-in-place.**

And I'll own the reversal: my first-turn "continue" was the right answer to the question you asked then, about a different product (the desk substrate missing its run core). The thesis moved during this conversation — the product is now a single local binary (`run / ps / inbox / land`) with SQLite underneath and commodity isolation. Against *that* product, the existing repo has the wrong shape, not just extra weight:

- It's a multi-daemon server substrate — Postgres + three daemons + JuiceFS + migrations just to start — while v1 is `brew install`, one binary, works offline in your repo. That's not a refactor distance; it's a different animal.
- Maybe 10–15% of the ~75k lines are on the new critical path. Carrying the other 85% costs you every day: CI, builds, docs describing a product you're no longer building, and the constant "how do we fit this into controld" gravity instead of "what does the product need."
- The genuinely valuable parts extract cleanly as code, not as running services: effectd's canonical-JSON + hash-chain + seal/verify (a Rust library), and the lease-CAS/generation-fencing logic with its tests (a few hundred lines of portable semantics). Vendor both, port their tests with them. This is ADR-0002's own doctrine — "vendor the algorithms, not the runtime" — applied to its own repo.
- Your instinct already said this ("less code to manage if stripped to its real value proposition"), and the research backed it: isolation, sync, and resume are now off-the-shelf; the differentiated code is small.

Guardrails so "new" doesn't become second-system disease:

1. Keep the old repo untouched as the parts shelf and reference — the fabric lives there and returns *only* when a migration trigger fires (mid-session follow-me, monorepo materialization SLO, physical fencing).
2. First commit in the new repo is the ADR: the four commands, the grant object, the gate predicates. Scope pinned before code.
3. One binary, SQLite, no required daemon, no SDKs, no surfaces beyond the CLI until the wedge proves.
4. Admission rule for every PR: does it serve `run`, `ps`, `inbox`, or `land`? If not, it waits.

Sleep on it — but that's the call: new shell, transplanted organs, old repo as the organ bank.

Fabric re-entry has four enumerated triggers (§6.5); nothing else reopens it.
- **Bounded open questions** (§17): approval-detection brittleness per agent version, scope-class defaults, Windows timing, and the final name — each owned before its phase starts.

Okay, please describe this to me using only product style descriptions as if you're trying to sell it to me like I'm an agentic developer. I use Claude code and codex and cursor on my laptop on servers and my phones. What's the value proposition here?

[195] ASSISTANT

[196] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Stratura

**Run your agents like a fleet, not like five toddlers you babysit.**

You already live this: Claude Code in two terminals, Codex in a third, a Cursor agent somewhere, maybe a cloud run you forgot about. Each one occasionally stops dead to ask "may I run this command?" — and sits there, silently, burning nothing but your calendar, while you're looking at the wrong window. Then at the end of the day you get five branches of unreviewed change and a queasy feeling about what actually happened in there.

Stratura is one binary that turns that circus into a managed fleet. You keep your agents, your subscriptions, your editor, your workflow. We add the four things nobody ships:

**Fire and forget.**
`stratura run -- claude "fix token refresh"` — the agent gets its own lane and keeps working when you close the terminal, drop SSH, or shut the lid. Runs are things that exist, not tabs you have to keep alive. `stratura ps` shows the whole fleet on one screen: working, waiting on you, stalled, done.

**One inbox instead of five terminals.**
Every "may I?", every stall, every finished task from every agent lands in a single queue. You answer from the inbox, not by hunting windows. The 2 a.m. version of you approves a package install in one keystroke and goes back to bed. (Today: terminal. Next: the same inbox on your phone — glance, approve, stop, from anywhere.)

**Lanes, not collisions.**
Each agent gets an isolated workspace scoped to exactly the files you assigned — it literally can't see the rest. Your working tree is never touched. Two agents can't clobber each other, and "YOLO mode" stops being a gamble because the blast radius is a sandbox you can throw away.

**Nothing lands without a receipt.**
Every merge comes with a sealed, tamper-evident account — every command it ran, every host it touched, everything it installed, which tests actually executed — produced by the infrastructure watching the agent, not by the agent grading its own homework. If anyone edits the record afterward, verification fails. You stop reviewing on vibes.

**The classics get caught automatically.**
Agent quietly edits the failing test so it passes? Refused — pinned tests can't be touched by the run that has to pass them. Agent wanders outside its lane? That work isn't silently merged *or* thrown away — it's split off into a proposal you can approve as its own task. Two runs racing for the same files? One holds the write authority; the other physically can't land.

**Work that travels.**
Wrap up on the laptop, pick the same task up on your server: the work moves as a branch with a handoff note, and where the agent supports it (Claude and Codex both do now), its own session resumes too. Your cloud runs — Codex cloud, Copilot, Claude web — show up in the same `ps` as your local ones, so "where is everything?" has one answer.

**Zero infrastructure.**
One binary, works offline, state in a local file, nothing phones home. `brew install`, `cd` into your repo, go. Uninstall leaves your repos exactly as they were.

---

**The pitch in one line:** you've already hired the agents — Stratura is the layer that lets you *manage* them: assign lanes, answer everything in one place, and merge with receipts instead of faith.

Ten-minute demo: three agents, three scopes, one inbox, one landing queue — including watching the gate catch a test-gamer and refuse a stale writer. If that demo doesn't map onto your Tuesday, don't buy it.

Much closer than anything else in this conversation — and closer than it was a year ago, so this deserves a straight answer, not a deflection. OpenHands pivoted in exactly this direction: [Agent Canvas](https://www.openhands.dev/blog/use-any-coding-agent-in-openhands-with-acp) now connects Claude Code, Codex, and Gemini CLI over ACP, runs parallel sessions in one view, keeps your existing subscriptions, moves backends between laptop, VM, and their cloud, and does scheduled automations. They even market it with the same sentence shape I used: "the platform layer around the agents rather than a replacement for them."

So yes: **the console half of the pitch — one screen, parallel runs, keep your agents — is now OpenHands' territory too.** Where the answer becomes "no" is the half that made this a kernel rather than a dashboard. Read their own docs closely: Canvas "sends messages and renders what comes back," and the external agent "manages its own LLM, tools, and execution." It's a conversation router with backend plumbing. What it doesn't have:

- **Lanes, enforced.** No scoped workspaces as capabilities, no refusal of overlapping claims, no lease with generations, no fencing. Two Canvas agents pointed at the same repo have exactly the collision story you have in two terminals today.
- **A landing gate.** Nothing sits between the agent's work and your branch checking footprint-within-scope, pinned tests untouched and passing, contracts unbroken, authority current — and nothing severs out-of-scope work into a proposal instead of merging or losing it. Closure in OpenHands is "the agent opened a PR."
- **Receipts.** Their enterprise tier lists "audit logs" — platform access logs, self-reported trajectories. That is categorically different from a tamper-evident, signed account of every command and network touch, produced by infrastructure watching the agent from outside, with a `verify` that fails if anyone edits a row. Their logs tell you what the platform saw the agent say; a sealed report is evidence that survives not trusting the agent, the platform, or the person who exported the log.
- **Authority that travels.** They move *sessions* between backends (genuinely nice). They don't move *write authority* with fencing — nothing prevents the abandoned copy from landing stale work.

The honest one-liner: **OpenHands built the switchboard; the thing you'd buy from me is the lock and the notary.** And notably, both products standardized on the same rail (ACP), which means the relationship isn't purely competitive — OpenHands' agent, and even Canvas-managed runs, can be workers *under* the gate: `stratura run -- openhands` is just another adapter, and their console could sit on top of our landing plane rather than beside it.

Two consequences I'd act on. First, the pitch must lead with the gate and the receipts — "run more agents from one screen" is no longer differentiating, and within a year every harness will claim it. The demo beats that matter are the ones OpenHands structurally can't do: the severed out-of-scope diff, the refused test-edit, the stale writer that can't land, and tamper-fails-verify. Second, this belongs in ADR-0007 as a named competitive fact with a drift tripwire — "if we're competing with Canvas on console features, we've drifted" — I'd add that paragraph to §4 and §15 if you want it recorded.

[206] ASSISTANT

You caught a real inconsistency, so let me stop pitching and just reason about your actual Tuesday.

You're right about the split: "close the laptop and it keeps working" is a thing a person wants. "Lock and notary" is a thing a CISO wants, and I slid into it because OpenHands ate the console pitch and I reached for what was left. That reach was the mistake — the notary is plumbing and enterprise upsell, not the product. Park it.

Now your scenario, taken seriously: two agents off the same base, one on Compose UI, one on server-side key attestation, fuzzy boundary, second one stomps some Compose files. And your two claims: (1) merge conflicts aren't the pain because LLMs resolve them fine, (2) the actual pain is *forgetting* — work you fired off and lost track of, stomps you only discover later, finished runs rotting because you were busy. I think both claims are correct, and they redefine the product.

Here's the thing your "why not let them wildly diverge" argument gets right, and what it quietly assumes. Optimistic divergence + LLM self-merge is genuinely the right default for your case — pre-declaring scopes is the wrong ergonomics when you don't know the split in advance, and locking is overkill for two agents. Anthropic's agents-arguing-and-burning-tokens mess is actually evidence *for* your position: coordination-during-work is waste; the cheap move is no coordination while working, reconciliation after. But notice what "let them figure out the merge" assumes: that somebody reliably *convenes* the merge — knows both branches exist, knows they overlap, remembers both task intents, runs the reconcile, checks it built, and lands both. Today that somebody is you, and you just told me you're the unreliable component. The failure mode of wild divergence isn't conflicts — it's that you are the merge queue, and you forget.

So for your use case the product is four dumb-sounding things, none of which is a lock:

1. **Runs that outlive your attention.** Fire it, close the lid, drop SSH, forget — it's still a thing with a state, not a dead tab.
2. **A list that nags.** Nothing silently rots: "compose-ui: done 3h ago, unlanded. attestation: still running. That refactor from Tuesday: stalled, nobody's looked." The inbox for you isn't approvals — it's *anti-forgetting*.
3. **Overlap radar, not overlap prevention.** No scopes declared. The system just watches what files each run actually touches and tells you the moment footprints cross: "both runs are editing NavGraph.kt." You can ignore it — usually you will, and that's fine. The point is stomps become a line you saw *now*, not a surprise at runtime next week.
4. **Merge as a run.** When both finish, "reconcile these two branches" is itself an agent job, spawned with both diffs *and both task prompts* as context — which is exactly what your ad-hoc LLM merge is missing today (the merging model usually doesn't know what each branch was *for*). It lands like any other run, behind whatever single check you care about ("the app still builds").

Replay your day with that: two launches, no scope ceremony. One glance at a "2 files overlap" line you ignore. When both finish, one `land` — Compose lands, a reconcile run absorbs the attestation branch on top, build check passes, done. Total human cost: two commands and one decision. Nothing forgotten, and nobody argued in a mailbox.

Where does that leave the stuff I over-sold? Demoted honestly: isolation-per-run stays but is invisible plumbing (it's just why runs can't corrupt each other mid-flight). Scopes become *optional and discovered* — the radar can suggest "next time, these were the natural lanes" instead of you guessing up front. The gate degrades to a landing checklist you opt into (pinned tests is the one piece with genuine consumer value, because test-gaming annoys everyone). Receipts become the glovebox: never pitched, occasionally consulted after a "wait, did it curl something weird" moment. Enterprise later turns those same dials up without changing your surface.

And to be straight about your "I'm still unsure the solution matters": the humbled version of the claim is falsifiable. If you never forget runs, always notice stomps yourself, and reliably convene the merges — you don't need this, and worktrees plus discipline is enough. The bet is only that the forgetting is structural (it is — you said it yourself: lazy, slow, that's everyone), and that memory + radar + a reconcile button is the smallest thing that fixes it. That's a smaller product than the spec I wrote yesterday — §2's buyer framing, mandatory scopes in §7, and the gate-first §8 all need softening to "optional strictness, divergence-by-default, reconcile-as-a-run" — and I'd rather amend the doc toward the smaller true thing than defend the bigger impressive one.

------------------------------------------------------------------------------

[209] ASSISTANT

You found it, and it's cleaner than anything we built up yesterday. Let me play it back in one sentence: **the thing that needs to teleport between your surfaces isn't the files or the workspace — it's the understanding.** Decisions made, questions left open, work in flight, and what it breaks downstream. Git already moves the files fine. Nothing moves the understanding, so *you* are the sync protocol, and you're a lossy one — that's the restart-or-flip-back-through-sessions cost you keep paying.

Notice what your story actually rules out. Checked-in transcripts: right instinct, wrong granularity — raw history eats context and still doesn't surface "there were three open questions and you never answered #2." Hot migration: you said you might need it, but look at your own example — the phone session's *architecture evolution* is what you wanted on the laptop, not its working tree. And locks/scopes/receipts: irrelevant to this failure, which happens with *one* agent and one forgetful human across two devices.

So the object is a **project journal** — not a chat log, a distilled ledger of four things:

- **Decisions**: "chose push over polling for attestation refresh, because X" — with a link back to the exact session and line it came from, so "flip back and forth to remember what was said" becomes one click to the source.
- **Open questions**: "never answered: should tokens rotate per launch?" — and these *age visibly*, so the Tuesday question nags you Thursday instead of dying in a phone session.
- **In flight**: laptop-Claude is mid-refactor on NavGraph, nine files dirty; server-Codex touched the mock server. Whether or not it's committed — this is your "whether they're in git or not" point; the journal watches workspaces, not just refs.
- **Impacts**: "the attestation change breaks the compose preview mocks" — the downstream effects you forget by evening.

Mechanically it's small, which is why I believe it: adapters tail the session files that already sit on disk (`~/.claude/projects/…`, `~/.codex/sessions/…`), a distill pass extracts those four categories (this is the Federation-over-Text move — share the distillate, never the transcript, which answers your context-cost objection by construction), and the journal syncs as a tiny ref through the GitHub remote you already use — a couple of KB of "what's true right now," no desk, no platform, no OpenHands. Every agent gets it injected at session start the way they all already consume repo files; every *human* gets the screen you described: lanes per surface, what each agent changed, the decision timeline, the question list.

And the moment from your story that matters most — "interject when agent 2 decides something I'd disagree with" — falls out of it: the journal holds decision D from the phone session; the laptop session's stream starts contradicting D; you get a line: *"server-codex is going with polling — conflicts with Tuesday's push decision (source: phone session)."* Not a block. A nudge, with provenance. Sometimes the contradiction is right because things changed — so it's radar, never a lock, same rule as before.

Two more things your version of the problem buys us. First, the wedge becomes **observe-only**: install it and it just watches — session files, repos, dirty trees across your machines — and builds the journal with *zero workflow change*. No `stratura run`, no worktree hygiene required; it even catches your same-folder disaster ("two agents share this directory and there are four uncommitted files — one of them is about to lose work") as a warning, not a demand. Orchestration, isolation, reconcile-runs — all become opt-in upgrades once the memory proves itself. Second, the neutrality argument finally attaches to something you *want*: Claude has memory, Cursor has memories, Codex has sessions — every one a per-vendor silo. The cross-agent, cross-surface, in-your-own-remote memory can only be built by the party that isn't one of the vendors.

Honest limits so we don't re-inflate: distillation quality is the whole product — if the decisions it extracts are mush, it gets ignored, so the journal must be a *human-editable file* with provenance links, not an oracle. Contradiction detection will have false positives; it's a nudge with a source link, tolerable. And this is a smaller, humbler thing than the fleet kernel — which is the third time this conversation has shrunk the product, and each shrink has been toward something you actually described wanting.

It does mean ADR-0007 is phased backwards for you: the knowledge plane (§13, parked in Phase 3) is the wedge, and the control plane (grants, gate, leases) is the later team-scale hardening. The four commands become `watch · journal · ps · run`, with `run` optional. I'd rewrite the spec around that inversion — say the word and I'll draft it as ADR-0008 so both shapes stay on record.

[212] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
You've half-answered it correctly — and the half you answered ("it needs to be live") is actually the *smaller* half. Let me split the question into the four jobs the journal does, because git cleanly gets one of them and structurally can't get the other three.

**Transport — git wins, use it.** The journal syncing as a tiny ref through your own remote is the right call: your infrastructure, no service, no platform. So the "why not git" question is really "why not *git hygiene*," and that's where it falls apart.

**The sensor — this is the real gap, and it's not about speed.** Your proposed fix is "the agent that makes a decision immediately checks it in and pushes." Notice what that solution's load-bearing component is: *an agent reliably noticing, mid-conversation, that something it just said constitutes a durable decision, interrupting its work, writing it down, committing, and pushing — every time, across three different vendors' agents, forever.* That's not a mechanism, it's a discipline — the same failure class as Claude Teams' lock files ("please claim tasks properly"), your own worktree hygiene ("I have bad hygiene I guess"), and every convention this conversation has watched fail. Decisions don't announce themselves; they happen as "yeah, go with push instead of polling" forty minutes into a session. The journal works because nobody has to remember anything: it *tails the session files that already exist on disk* and extracts decisions from what was actually said. Observe-by-default versus publish-by-discipline. Git can carry the report; it cannot be the witness.

**Timeliness — you're right, but sharper than you said it.** It's not just that commits lag by hours; it's that *every event you care about exists before any commit by design*. The decision exists at utterance time. The open question exists the moment it's asked and not answered. The stomp exists at file-write time, in the uncommitted window — which is exactly where your lost-dirty-files disasters live, and no amount of commit hygiene covers the gap *between* commits, because the gap is the hazard. A watcher on session streams and working trees publishes at event time (and yes, it can push the journal ref through git the second it updates — transport git, trigger not-git).

**Format — transcripts are the wrong artifact even if perfectly synced.** You already said why for context cost, but it's worse: if agent 2 reads agent 1's raw 400KB JSONL, it re-derives "what was decided, what's open" from scratch — and so does agent 3, and so do you, on a phone, never. N readers × M sessions of re-derivation, burning exactly the tokens you complained about with agent-teams. Distill once, share the two-kilobyte answer — that's the Federation-over-Text result in miniature (their measured win was *fewer* tokens, not more). Plus transcripts carry secrets and noise you don't want in repo history. The check-in instinct is right; the thing checked in must be the distillate with provenance links back to the source lines.

**The reader — the interject moment needs something watching, not something filed.** Even with a perfectly live, perfectly distilled `decisions.md` in the repo, agent 2 consults it once, at session start. Your killer scenario — agent 2 starts contradicting Tuesday's decision mid-session — is only catchable by something comparing agent 2's *live stream* against the decision list and nudging you (or it) *now*. Git delivers documents to processes that choose to pull and re-read them; it doesn't watch anybody. "Poll the repo for updates" puts the discipline burden back on the agent, mid-session, with token cost per poll — the exact pathology you just watched agent-teams get roasted for.

So the clean answer to "why doesn't git alone solve this": **git is the wire; it was never the witness, the distiller, or the reader.** And your Anthropic reference is the empirical proof — their JSON-files-in-a-shared-directory design *is* the "just check things in" architecture, and its published failure list (silent non-compliance, stale task states, nobody notices a dead teammate) is what discipline-based publication does at scale. One caveat in your favor: vendors are converging on agents auto-writing memory files (CLAUDE.md updates and the like), and that raises the floor — those files are just more sources for the watcher to ingest. The part that stays yours is the part no vendor will build: cross-agent, cross-surface, observing everything, filing for the human.

[215] ASSISTANT

Right — and that refinement matters more than it might seem, because **findings are the most valuable and most-lost category**, worse than decisions. A decision at least *might* get written into a doc by a diligent agent. A benchmark number spoken mid-session — "ran the load test, p95 came back at 340ms, that's rough" — has no natural home in git at all. It's not code, it's not a doc anyone planned to write, it dies with the session. And it's the *expensive* kind of knowledge: it came from actually running something. When it's lost, the other agent doesn't just lack it — it re-runs the whole discovery loop to re-learn it, which is the token-burn you keep circling back to. (This is precisely what the Federation-over-Text paper measured, by the way: the win from sharing distilled *learned insights* was fewer tokens and better outcomes because agents stopped re-deriving each other's discoveries.)

So the journal's taxonomy generalizes: the unit isn't "decision," it's **any change to the shared understanding of the project**. Five subtypes, now complete:

- **Decisions** — chosen (push over polling)
- **Findings** — learned or measured (p95 is 340ms; that API silently truncates at 1MB; the flaky test is `AuthRoundTrip`)
- **Questions** — asked and still open
- **In-flight** — happening right now, committed or not
- **Impacts** — consequences ("attestation change breaks the compose mocks")

And your causal chain — "because of that, another agent should change its approach" — is the same alarm mechanism as the contradiction nudge, pointed at facts instead of decisions: the journal knows laptop-Codex's plan assumes fast responses; phone-Claude just measured 340ms; that plan section is now flagged *stale, with a link to the actual test output*, so the agent (or you) verifies the number instead of trusting a summary. Findings need that provenance more than decisions do, plus two bits of context decisions don't: *where* it was measured (emulator vs your server changes what 340ms means) and *when* (findings get superseded; the journal invalidates, it doesn't accumulate contradictory truths).

It also seals the argument you were testing two messages ago: better git hygiene could plausibly capture decisions — an agent can be prompted to write ADRs. It will essentially never capture findings, because nobody, human or agent, experiences a mid-session measurement as "a thing to publish." Only something watching the stream does.

[218] ASSISTANT

Don't hedge on this one — it's not secondary, it's the half that makes the whole thing coherent. Here's the reframe: the human isn't a *viewer* of this knowledge graph, the human is a *node* in it — and today you're the most overloaded node in the system. You're the only entity that has been present in every session on every surface, so you're currently performing the sync role by memory. Everything we've discussed — forgotten decisions, lost findings, restart-or-flip-back — is just the symptom of that node dropping packets. The journal doesn't add the human to the graph; it *relieves* the human of being its only working edge.

Your "even with perfect git history" point is exactly right, and here's why it stays right: commit history is an output log, not project state. It answers "what changed," never "who's on what, where, what's decided, what's blocked, what's next." Reading it to figure out where the project is at is like reading every bank transaction to learn your balance. The journal is the balance.

And the anti-pattern you named — asking agents for status, "did you fetch from main?" — deserves to be killed by name, because it violates the same law everything else in this conversation violated before we fixed it: it's *ask-by-discipline* instead of *observe-by-default*, just pointed at reads instead of writes. Interrogating an agent for status costs tokens, spins up a conversation, and gets you a self-report from a narrator who only knows its own session and is optimistic by temperament. Meanwhile "did you fetch from main" is literally an observable fact — the watcher knows each workspace's base and how far behind it is. Status should be a *displayed property*, never an interview.

So the human surface is one glance answering six things nobody is asked for: **who** (which agents, which surfaces), **where** (on what base, N commits behind), **what** (footprints — the areas each is actually touching), **decided** (last few, with source links), **learned** (recent findings), **open** (questions aging, waiting on *you*). That last one matters most: some entries only a human can resolve, and they should route to a small human-queue — the two or three things blocking agents on your judgment — separate from the glanceable rest. And it's bidirectional: you make decisions too, sometimes just in your head between sessions. The journal being an editable file means you're a first-class *writer* — your decision gets echoed to every agent the same way theirs get echoed to you.

On your "maybe not possible" hedge — split it. The observational layer (bases, footprints, dirty trees, who's-where) is plain fs-and-git fact, zero doubt. The extraction layer carries the quality risk we already flagged. The genuinely hard part is neither — it's *ranking*: showing five things that matter instead of fifty that don't, because the failure mode of every status surface is becoming a feed you stop reading. That's why the split is push-versus-pull: pull is the glance, whenever you want it; push is reserved for human-blocking items only. If it ever pings you for something that didn't need you, it's dying.

The acceleration argument closes it: agents parallelized the work, but nobody parallelized *your situational awareness* — so you're the serial section, Amdahl's-law style, and every "wait, what the f*** is going on" costs a context switch plus an interrogation round per session. The journal turns that from O(N sessions to interview) into O(one glance). That's where the speed actually is — not in the agents typing faster, but in you never having to rebuild the picture.

The history reads like a controlled experiment in exactly the failure we've been describing. Bottom to top: Phase 0/1/2 extensions land ([#2](https://github.com/maceip/stratura/pull/2), [#3](https://github.com/maceip/stratura/pull/3)) — then the very next substantive commit is "Ship **Phase 4/5** fabric and multi-surface conflict inbox." Phase 3 skipped, Phase 2's actual purpose (run a workload) never revisited. From there it's a flawless procession outward: security hardening ([#5](https://github.com/maceip/stratura/pull/5)), tool plane + CRIU hooks ([#6](https://github.com/maceip/stratura/pull/6)), Android Compose operator desk ([#7](https://github.com/maceip/stratura/pull/7)), surface contracts ([#8](https://github.com/maceip/stratura/pull/8)), design system, conformance suites, the apk_bionic Claude wrap, the iOS Safari-OAuth wrap, native chrome for every OS ([#10](https://github.com/maceip/stratura/pull/10)), embed SDKs in seven languages ([#11](https://github.com/maceip/stratura/pull/11)). Fifty commits. Not one touches "launch an agent." And here's the damning part: every individual commit is *good* — CI enforcement, golden fixtures, evidence files, honest exit codes. Local coherence, global drift. No single session ever did anything wrong; the absence was nobody's task.

And you're right that the ADR discipline didn't just fail to prevent this — it demonstrates precisely *which layer* discipline can cover. The decision layer worked: ADR-0002 through 0006 exist, they're articulate, they even contain the correct plan ("Phase 2 is the enterprise bar; don't block on Phase 3"). What didn't exist was the **state layer**: nothing tracked that "Phase 2 exit: run a real workload" had been in-flight *zero times* while eleven PRs landed around it, and nothing observed completion — you assumed done-ness, weren't checking PRs, and each agent's session self-reported success at its local task, truthfully. ADRs made the drift articulate. They couldn't make it *visible*. A journal's glance-screen would have shown one line by roughly commit fifteen: *"core workload runner: never in-flight · 6 surface PRs landed since"* — and that line is the entire product, compressed.

The recursive kicker: this conversation is the invoice for not having it. Your first message yesterday was, functionally, "hire an agent for a full session of archaeology to reconstruct project state and tell me if we should start over." My "impressive perimeter around a missing center" wasn't insight — it was the glance, delivered fifty commits and one restart-deliberation too late, at the price of a day. That's the O(N-sessions-of-interrogation) → O(one-glance) argument, run on ourselves.

I'll also own my part of the pattern: ADR-0007's drift guards — "every PR must state which command it serves" — are *more discipline*, the same class of fix that just demonstrably failed in this very repo. The journal inverts it: don't make agents declare alignment, observe the plan's items and show which ones have never moved. That's the final nail for inverting the spec: knowledge plane first.

One practical closer: this repo is the perfect first eval fixture. Feed the watcher this commit history plus whatever session files survive in `~/.claude/projects` from those agents, and the acceptance test writes itself — does it flag "the center is missing" while the center is still three PRs from being buried? It already has ground truth: we know exactly when the drift started, because we just read it.

Good instinct to demand the academic backing here, because the answer has a sharp edge: **a semantic code graph alone could not have caught it — and there's a 30-year-old technique that catches exactly it, whose one missing input is the thing we just designed.**

Here's the edge. A code knowledge graph — [LogicLens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.10773) included — is a graph of *what exists*: files, classes, functions, plus LLM-enriched functional abstractions (domain entities, operations, workflows). Every query in that whole product wave (perplexity-style repo chat, agentic code search) is shaped "explain/navigate what's here." Your failure was an **absence** — a node that never came into existence. You can't see a hole in a picture of present things, and you can't ask about it either, because asking requires already suspecting ("in my head I had all the pieces, I just wasn't keeping track" — the absence was an unknown-unknown). A repo graph of Stratura would have given you a gorgeous, navigable map of the Android surface you liked looking at. That's arguably *worse* — rich maps of what exists actively distract from what doesn't.

The mechanism that sees absence is **reflexion models** — [Murphy, Notkin & Sullivan, FSE '95](https://www.cs.ubc.ca/~murphy/papers/rm/fse95.html), later formalized in TSE and validated on Excel (millions of LOC) and NetBSD. The setup: the engineer states a high-level model of what the system *should* be, maps it to source, and the tool computes three edge classes — convergences, divergences, and **absences: things present in the intended model and missing in the implementation**. Absence is a first-class computed output, not a query you have to think to ask. "Phase 2 core: workload runner — ABSENT" is literally the kind of edge this technique emits.

So why isn't reflexion modeling ambient in every repo already? Because its intent-model input had to be **hand-drawn and hand-mapped**, and nobody maintains that. And that's the click: **the journal is the automatically-maintained intent model** the technique always lacked. Decisions, planned items, phases (your ADRs already contain them!), open questions — accumulated by watching sessions, with provenance. The stack assembles cleanly, each layer proven separately:

- **Intent graph** — the journal (auto-maintained; the novel piece, possible only because something is finally witnessing the sessions where intent gets uttered)
- **Reality graph** — git, commits, PR footprints, dirty trees; later upgradeable to a LogicLens-grade semantic graph
- **Mapping** — classic reflexion's manual pain, now automatable: path globs first, LLM-assisted traceability linking where paths aren't enough (LogicLens's functional-abstraction layer is exactly what makes intent-node → code-region linking tractable)
- **The diff** — reflexion semantics: converged / diverged / absent, recomputed continuously

And the human surface is one map with two overlays, which merges your two questions from this message: tiles are intent items, colored by observed reality — Stratura's would have shown *"run a workload: 0 sessions, 0 commits, 0 PRs"* glowing gray next to *"surfaces: 11 PRs"* burning hot — and the same tiles carry the **allocation overlay** (which agent's footprint is on which region), answering "who's working on what, where" from the same data.

On your scope-creep worry — the guardrail is that v1 needs a table join, not a graph database. The intent graph is dozens of nodes; reality is git facts the watcher already collects; mapping is globs plus an LLM call when ambiguous. Kuzu/AST/symbol-level graphs are a **pluggable enrichment layer** that upgrades mapping precision and adds impact edges later — that's the Lego-block abstraction you're reaching for: three swappable layers (intent / mapping / reality) with the reflexion diff as the fixed contract between them.

Which also gives last message's eval a formal spec: feed the differ this repo's history with ADR-0002's own phase table as the intent model, and it must emit "absence: Phase 2 exit criterion — run a workload" by roughly commit 15, while the surface tiles fill in around it. The intent model was sitting in `adr/` the whole time. What was missing was the machine that never stops comparing it to reality — and the reason it could never exist before is that nothing was watching where intent actually gets spoken: the sessions.

Exactly — and that's the final crystallization of the whole thing: **people aren't restarting because their code is bad; they're restarting because restarting is the only known way to reset intent-reality divergence to zero.** A fresh repo is the one moment when what you mean and what exists are trivially identical. Everything after that drifts silently, and since nobody has an instrument for the drift, the only options are archaeology (what you paid me for yesterday) or the reset button. The product is the instrument.

And there's a recursion I'm not going to let happen: this session, right now, is exactly the kind of session whose decisions die — it's 9:50 PM on a Saturday, we just made half a dozen real decisions across three hours of voice notes, and tomorrow's session on some other surface won't know any of it. So I'm going to practice what we just designed and write it down as ADR-0008 — short decision record, not another 600-line spec — including the session's own journal entries (decisions, findings, open questions) as the appendix, as the first dogfood.

Captured. `adr/ADR-0008-intent-reality-inversion.md` is committed alongside ADR-0007 on [PR #14](https://github.com/maceip/stratura/pull/14) — short decision record, not another spec: the thesis (restarts are unmeasured divergence), the locked object model (journal → intent graph → reality graph → mapping → reflexion diff with absence as a first-class output), the six laws we learned the hard way, the observe-only wedge, and what got demoted to team-phase strictness.

The appendix is the part I'd look at tomorrow: this session's actual journal entries — seven decisions, five findings with sources, three open questions. That's the first dogfood, done by hand exactly once: the next session you open, on any surface, with any agent, can be pointed at that file and know that D1 inverted the phasing, that F4 says merge conflicts aren't the pain, and that Q3 (where the journal ref lives) is still yours to answer. Which is the product working, manually — the last time it should ever have to be manual.

[245] ASSISTANT

[246] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
That's the strongest counter-argument in this whole conversation, and it's half right in a way that makes the other half more interesting. Restart-as-strategy is real, it *is* why no drift instrument has caught on, and faster token delivery strengthens it. But look closely at the phrase doing the heavy lifting: "getting to what you believe is a knowledge equilibrium." Equilibrium with *what*?

A restart doesn't reset divergence to zero. It resets *reality* to zero and truncates *intent* to whatever you can still articulate — what's in your head plus what made it into the new prompt. Every decision whose reasoning you forgot, every finding earned by contact with reality (the p95 that came back at 340ms, the API that silently truncates, the edge case from week two) — the restart doesn't recover those, it *ratifies* their loss. And the loss is invisible by construction: you can't miss what you forgot you knew. The fresh repo feels like equilibrium precisely because there's no artifact left to disagree with you. Each restart is a lossy compression cycle on the project's accumulated knowledge, and the compression artifact is "agents cheerfully rebuild without the constraint, and you either pay the discovery cost a second time or ship the bug."

Now run the economics forward with your own trend. Tokens get faster and cheaper → reality becomes abundant and disposable. When one input to production collapses in cost, value migrates to the complement — and the complement of cheap regeneration is *the thing you regenerate from*. Faster tokens also mean, as you said, faster divergence ("nobody is careful"), which means more restart cycles per month, which means more loss events per month. The trend you're describing doesn't erode the instrument's value — it concentrates all remaining value into the one layer the trend can't cheapen: durable, transmissible intent. Specs regenerate code; nothing regenerates the findings you didn't write down.

Which flips the product's relationship to restarts entirely: don't compete with the restart pattern — *complete* it. If the journal survives across resets — and it does, because it's not the code, it's the distilled decisions-findings-questions ledger in your own remote — then "start over" stops being amnesia and becomes a **compile step**: reality is the build artifact, the journal is the source, and a restart is re-materializing the artifact from source with today's faster toolchain. That's the endgame the industry is stumbling toward without naming it: code-as-output, intent-as-source-of-truth. Your 3-to-10-agents-going-crazy workflow doesn't change at all; what changes is that the tenth restart starts from twenty-three findings and nine reasoned decisions instead of from vibes and residual memory.

And that yields the concrete feature for exactly your persona — the restart-lover's tool rather than the restart-scold's: a **restart manifest**. At the moment you reach for the reset button, one screen: "you are about to discard a reality containing 23 findings and 9 decisions; 6 of them appear nowhere in your new spec — carry them, or consciously drop them." Loss becomes a choice instead of an accident. That's radar-not-locks applied to the biggest destructive operation in the workflow.

The honest boundary, so this stays calibrated: for projects below the complexity threshold — where the full intent genuinely fits in a prompt and nothing was *earned* from reality contact — you're right, restart wins, no instrument needed, and that's a real and large class of disposable software. The market is the other class: projects that have touched reality (users, data, perf, integrations) and accumulated knowledge that exists nowhere but in dead sessions and your head. Agents are moving onto exactly those projects right now, at exactly the moment token speed makes their divergence faster than anyone's attention. That intersection — earned knowledge times cheap divergence — is where restarts quietly stop being free, and where the source file for the recompile becomes the most valuable file in the repo.

Before writing, three places where I'm going to deviate from what we said earlier, because parts of it don't survive contact with implementation — noting them up front per your no-glaze rule:

1. **"In-flight" is not a journal entry type** — it's a computed *status* on intent entries (from footprints/commits). ADR-0008's five types were slightly wrong; the extracted types are `decision | finding | question | intent | impact`, and in-flight/done/absent are what the differ computes. 
2. **The "live diff of intent and reality graphs" is a join, not a graph engine.** Entries + evidence links + a status algebra recomputed on change does everything the reflexion diff requires; a graph DB adds nothing at this scale. The doc says this plainly.
3. **Mid-session agent nudges hit real physics**: Claude has hooks that can inject context next-turn; Codex mostly doesn't. The doc specifies a delivery matrix with the invariant "a nudge that can't reach the agent must reach the human" — that's the actual capability landscape, not a fallback stub.

Writing the founding spec now, self-contained for lift-out into the new repo.

[257] ASSISTANT

`JOURNAL_SPEC.md` is written, committed to the branch on [PR #14](https://github.com/maceip/stratura/pull/14), and self-contained for lift-out — no references that assume the old repo.

Where the hard edges got real answers instead of stubs:

- **Multi-machine sync with no server**: append-only, ULID-named entry files on an orphan `stratura/journal` branch — conflict-free by filename construction, fetchable by any agent with plain git. Custom ref namespaces were rejected explicitly (they die in half the tooling).
- **Extraction trust**: no quote, no entry — every journal item carries a verbatim redacted quote and source span, schema-validated, with a hard cost budget (≤2% of observed session tokens) metered in `status`. Failure parks the slice and goes red; nothing guess-parses.
- **The absence rule is relative, not wall-clock**: an intent goes `absent` when five sibling intents gained evidence while it gained none — that's the rule that catches "11 surface PRs, core never touched" without false-alarming a paused project.
- **Mid-session nudges**: a per-agent delivery matrix reflecting actual physics (Claude hook injects next-turn; Codex routes through the human; wrapped PTYs get a line at prompt boundary) under one invariant — a nudge that can't reach the agent must reach the human.
- **The graphs are a join**: entries + evidence links + a status algebra, recomputed on change. The doc says this plainly and leaves semantic-code-graph enrichment behind a measured trigger (unmapped-intent rate >20%).
- **Restart manifest**: disposition pass against the new spec (covered/missing/contradicts), a ≤4KB `SEED.md` carry-kit, and `init --carry` genesis import — with dropped knowledge *recorded as a dated choice*, which is the whole point.

Two corrections to our own earlier thinking are baked in rather than papered over: "in-flight" became a computed status instead of a fifth entry type, and the phone story is honestly "journal branch renders on GitHub mobile + webhook push" rather than a promised app. The three acceptance fixtures are the load-bearing ones only — including running the differ over the agentdesk repo's own history, where the ground truth is the failure we lived: your CRIU-and-networked-FS era was an attempt to force agents to share *state*; this spec ships the thing that actually needed sharing, which was the *knowledge*.

[258] USER
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
why spec out  multi-machine sync with no server?  im not opposed to it but just providing  a public sync instance should be cheap given the size of these journals / graphs / etc should be tiny compared to git or docker right ?

[259] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------


[260] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Fair question, and cost was never the reason — you're right that these journals are kilobytes and a hosted instance would cost effectively nothing. The serverless choice is about four things that have nothing to do with hosting economics, plus one thing a server genuinely does better, which deserves to be in the spec rather than argued away.

**The real reasons git-remote is the substrate:**

1. **It's the only endpoint every agent environment can already reach.** This is the decisive one, and it comes straight from the landscape we mapped earlier: 2026 agent sandboxes run deny-by-default egress — Copilot's firewall, Codex cloud allowlists, srt's proxy, Docker sandbox network policies. The one domain guaranteed to be allowlisted *and* credentialed in every one of those environments is the repo's remote, because the sandbox exists to work on the repo. A sync service is a second endpoint and a second credential that has to be threaded through every firewall, secret store, and CI config — per project, per surface, forever. `git fetch origin stratura/journal` works today in every context an agent can exist in, including ones we don't control.

2. **The journal is the most sensitive artifact in the system.** It's distilled project intent — decisions, findings, quotes from your sessions. A hosted default means the first command exfiltrates exactly that to someone else's box, which resurrects the same consent problem we flagged for the R2 fabric a day ago, and it poisons the later enterprise story ("your knowledge, in your remote" is the neutrality moat).

3. **Zero new liveness dependencies.** The glance is a trust product; a sync server that's down makes the journal silently stale everywhere, which kills trust precisely where it's load-bearing. The git remote is already the project's existing liveness dependency — we add none. And local-first survives: the laptop on a plane journals fine and syncs like code does.

4. **Authz for free.** Who may read/write the journal = who may read/write the repo. A server needs accounts, tokens, ACLs — the exact policy rathole you banned.

**What a server genuinely does better — conceded:** latency and the phone write-path. Git polling gives ~30-second propagation; real-time nudges across machines want push. And answering an inbox question from your phone wants an HTTP POST, not a git commit from a mobile UI.

The resolution is the standard one, and it's now in the spec: **git stays the source of truth; a relay is an optional accelerator that only carries signals** — "journal changed, entries X/Y" events and inbox answers, stateless, no journal content stored. If the relay dies, everything degrades to poll speed; nothing breaks, nothing forks. That relay is exactly the thing you can host publicly for near-zero cost — and notably, it's also the natural monetization seam later: git is the protocol, the hosted relay + team dashboard + phone write-path is the product. Same move GitHub ran on git itself.

I've amended `JOURNAL_SPEC.md` §4 with the relay subsection and the degradation rule so this decision doesn't live only in the chat.

ok so before i butted in and asked about multi-machine sync with no server what was your soluition / what was your thinking there

[271] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------


[272] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
The design I'd written into §4 before your question was this, and the thinking behind it in the order it actually happened:

**The problem as I framed it:** N machines each run a watcher; each watcher writes entries extracted from local sessions; every machine, every agent, and every fresh clone must converge on one coherent journal. The classic answers are a central server/DB, CRDTs, or merging a shared file — and under your rules (one binary, no infra, simple-but-hard over Rube Goldberg), all three smelled wrong: a server is new infrastructure and a new credential; CRDTs are heavy machinery for what is fundamentally a log; and two watchers appending to one `journal.md` is guaranteed merge-conflict hell.

**The move: make the concurrency problem not exist, by construction.** Three interlocking choices:

1. **Append-only, one file per entry, ULID-named.** Two machines can never conflict because they never write the same file — every new entry is a new file with a globally unique, time-sortable name. Anything that looks like mutation (edit, confirm, supersede, answer, attach evidence) is modeled as a *new* entry referencing the old one, event-sourced style. So merging two machines' work is always a union of distinct filenames — the one merge git performs with zero conflict potential. The human/agent-readable `journal.md` and `digest.md` are derived artifacts each watcher regenerates deterministically from the entry set; entries are truth, rollups are cache, so even a race on the rollup is harmless.

2. **An orphan branch (`stratura/journal`) as the meeting point.** Sync is nothing but `pull --rebase` + `push` on that branch, triggered on change. Non-fast-forward just means "another machine added files"; rebase unions them; push. No locks, no vector clocks, no protocol — because choice 1 already removed divergent edits from the space of possible states. I explicitly considered and rejected a custom ref namespace (`refs/stratura/*`) — cleaner-looking, but default fetch refspecs skip custom refs and half the tooling ignores them, which would break the property I cared most about: *any agent in any clone can read the journal with plain `git fetch origin stratura/journal`*. And I rejected putting it on working branches because the journal must never appear in PRs or code history.

3. **Piggyback the project's existing remote** as transport, inheriting identity, authorization, encryption, and offline semantics for free — journal syncs exactly like code does: written locally on a plane, pushed when connectivity returns.

The underlying idea was the same one that ran through the whole celld/leases discussion, taken one step further: there, "exactly one owner per object, enforced by CAS" — here, *no object is ever shared for writing at all*, so the journal needs no authority mechanism whatsoever. Don't build coordination; construct the data model so coordination has nothing to do.

What that design traded away — and what your question then extracted — was propagation latency (poll-speed, ~30s, versus push) and a phone write-path (answering an inbox item wants an HTTP POST, not a mobile git commit). At spec time I'd accepted those silently as the cost of zero infrastructure; your "a sync instance would be cheap" was correct pressure on exactly that silent trade, which is why the resolution became the §4.1 relay: git remains the substrate and source of truth, and a stateless signal-only relay buys back the two things a server is genuinely better at, with the degradation rule that keeps it an accelerator rather than a dependency.

[273] USER
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Multi-machine sync with no server: append-only, ULID-named entry files on an orphan stratura/journal branch — conflict-free by filename construction, fetchable by any agent with plain git. Custom ref namespaces were rejected explicitly (they die in half the tooling).

[274] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------


[275] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
That's the bullet, and it compresses exactly what I walked through last turn. The one clause in it I asserted without showing receipts is the parenthetical — "custom ref namespaces die in half the tooling" — so here's that made concrete, since it's the load-bearing rejection:

- **Default refspecs skip them.** Every clone fetches `+refs/heads/*` — branches. A `refs/stratura/journal` ref is never fetched unless every machine, sandbox, and CI job edits its refspec config. That's per-environment configuration discipline multiplied across every surface — the exact class of requirement the design bans. A branch needs nothing: `git fetch origin stratura/journal` works in a stock clone because branch names resolve through the default namespace.
- **Agent ergonomics.** Agents reliably produce `git fetch origin <branch-name>`; they do not reliably produce `git fetch origin refs/stratura/journal:refs/stratura/journal` refspec syntax. The journal's whole reader story is "any agent, any clone, plain git" — the custom ref makes the happy path a syntax quiz.
- **Host UI invisibility.** GitHub's web and mobile UI render branches; custom ref namespaces are unviewable there entirely. The v1 phone *read* path is literally "journal.md renders on GitHub mobile" — a custom ref deletes that surface outright.
- **Platform semantics are inconsistent.** Hosts reserve and special-case namespaces (`refs/pull/*` read-only on GitHub, `refs/for/*` magic on Gerrit, similar carve-outs on GitLab), selective mirrors and platform APIs enumerate heads and tags, and fork/PR flows ignore everything else. Branches are the one ref class with universally boring behavior.

So the orphan branch costs one aesthetic annoyance — it appears in the branch list — and buys default-fetchability, agent-typeable access, mobile rendering, and host-neutral semantics. The custom namespace is the cleaner-looking choice that fails quietly in exactly the environments (sandboxes, CI, fresh clones, phones) the journal exists to reach.
