Git-only isn't the degraded mode; it's the **default and complete** mode, and a status warning should only ever appear if a relay was *configured* and then died — never for simply not having one. Fixing both now.

Confirmed and tightened. The spec now states it in two places, unambiguously:

- **§4 gained an explicit "Baseline sync protocol" bullet**: each watcher pushes the journal branch on local change (debounced ~5s) and fetch+rebases on a ≤30s interval and before materializing `context.md` — and that alone is the complete, fully-supported mode. Extraction, differ, glance, inbox, and manifest all function with nothing but the repo's remote.
- **§4.1 reworded**: "A relay is never required" — running without one is *normal operation, not a degraded state, and produces no warnings*. A status note appears only if a relay was configured and then became unreachable.

The framing inversion mattered: before this edit, §4.1's "falls back to poll-speed with a status note" could have been read as relay-less mode being a nagging fallback. Now the hierarchy is explicit — git-only is the design, the relay is an accelerator someone may bolt on. Pushed to [PR #14](https://github.com/maceip/stratura/pull/14).

Review done — findings first, then I applied the repairs. The headline: I found one genuine design bug that broke my own core invariant, one missing defense that's arguably the most serious gap in the doc, and four places where we were high on the idea. Fixes below, then the diffs.

**A. A real bug: the spec violated its own conflict-freedom claim.** §3.2 persists `status`, `evidence`, and `disposition` *inside entry files*, and §7.1 has the differ "attaching" evidence to entries — but the differ on your server will attach evidence to an entry your laptop created. Two differs appending to the same file = content conflict, which §4 swore was impossible. Fix: entry files become **immutable**; everything that happens later (evidence links, confirm/reject, supersede, answer, disposition) is a separate append-only **event file** in `events/`; status is *never persisted* — every watcher computes it locally from entries+events. Also fixed the bootstrap race (two machines creating unrelated orphan roots) and added `redact` (the one sanctioned history rewrite, since append-only + git otherwise makes a leaked secret immortal).

**B. Missing entirely: the journal is a prompt-injection amplifier and had no defense.** Text an agent reads from a hostile webpage → extractor distills it into a "finding" → injected into every future session on every surface. That's a cross-agent injection chain we built and didn't defend. Fix: extractor now records `utterance_by` (user / assistant / tool_result); tool_result-sourced quotes are tainted and rendered as fenced data only; context.md carries a fixed "memory, not instructions" preamble; imperative-shaped entries need human confirm before context inclusion.

**C. High on the idea — demoted four things.** (1) The LLM contradiction detector: v1 now only marks `possible-contradiction` via cheap tag/topic co-surfacing with both quotes — a human confirms `contradicted`; semantic judgment never auto-flips status. (2) The absence rule would have fired an alarm storm on day 2 of any archaeology-seeded repo (40 TODO-derived intents × K=5 siblings); absence is now restricted to *confirmed or high-confidence session-sourced* intents. (3) The nudge matrix over-promised: "next user turn" is ≈ never during hours-long autonomous runs, so the matrix now states plainly that during autonomy, nudges are human-directed for every vendor. (4) The 2KB context cap didn't survive arithmetic (15 decisions alone ≈ 2.1KB); it's 4KB with a deterministic truncation order. Plus one simplification: `impact` is gone as a type — it's a causal finding with an `affects` field; four types, simpler extractor.

**D. Misinterpretation and honesty fixes.** Added the decision-vs-intent disambiguator ("a decision *closes* alternatives; an intent *opens* work — one sentence can yield both"). Fixture 2's ground truth was circular — I wrote the ADR-0008 labels myself, so testing the extractor against them tests agreement-with-me; labels must now be human-ratified, and there's an explicit **go/no-go kill criterion** (precision ≥0.9, recall ≥0.75 within five prompt iterations, or the premise fails — written down instead of "tune forever"). And the cloud-sensor trigger row now admits that for a phone-heavy user it likely fires immediately.

**E. Reviewed and deliberately kept:** the orphan branch (survived the ref-namespace re-litigation), BYO-extractor via your own agent CLIs, the 2%/200k budget, git-only default sync. No changes there.

Applying the edits now.

All repairs are committed and pushed to [PR #14](https://github.com/maceip/stratura/pull/14) — the review pass changed 100 lines, removed 44. The structural summary of what the spec now says differently:

- **Entries are immutable; everything after birth is an event file** (`events/<ulid>.yaml`: evidence, confirm, reject, supersede, answer, disposition). Status is computed by each watcher and never persisted to the branch — the conflict-freedom claim is now actually true instead of asserted. The spec names its own prior bug in place, so the reasoning survives.
- **Four entry types**, with the decision-vs-intent disambiguator ("a decision closes alternatives; an intent opens work").
- **§6.5 injection stance**: `utterance_by` taint tracking, the memory-not-instructions preamble, human confirm for imperative-shaped entries.
- **Absence has an eligibility guard** (confirmed or ≥0.8 session-sourced; archaeology entries only after confirm) so the day-two alarm storm can't happen.
- **Contradiction is a co-surfaced pair a human confirms**, never an LLM-set status.
- **Nudge matrix carries the autonomy honesty note** — turn-boundary injection is the ceiling, not real-time steering.
- **`redact` exists**, bootstrap races are handled, context is 4KB with deterministic truncation, and fixture 2 has human-ratified ground truth plus a written kill criterion (precision ≥0.9 / recall ≥0.75 within five instruction iterations, or the premise fails).

Worth noting what the exercise demonstrated: the worst bug — a spec that swore "no content conflict is possible" while specifying differ-written fields on shared files — sat through four commits of careful editing until a deliberate adversarial re-read. That's your review question doing exactly what the journal is supposed to do for code: the absence of scrutiny, not the presence of errors, is where drift lives. The spec is in materially better shape than it was an hour ago; the remaining honest risk is unchanged and named in §10 — extraction fidelity, which no amount of document review can settle. Only the fixtures can.

>
> 1. **Baseline.** Make the first commit of the current state. Then run `init` on this repo itself so the tool journals its own development from commit 1. Push journal + code when the remote exists.
> 2. **Independent verification — trust nothing in the build report.** Re-run all tests and the three §10 acceptance gates from a clean clone. Add three adversarial checks if absent: (a) two clones appending entries/events concurrently → rebase-union with zero conflicts and zero lost files; (b) the I7 gate rejecting a deliberately fabricated entry (no verbatim quote in the source span); (c) §6.5 behavior — a tool_result-sourced imperative entry must be withheld from context.md pending confirm. Produce the `map --html` artifact showing the workload-runner intent `absent` while surface intents show evidence. > 5. **Supervised dogfood.** Turn the watcher on for [list your 2–3 active real repos]. Before starting, declare the week-one metrics in the journal: extraction spend vs. budget, human confirm-vs-reject ratio on extracted entries, fraction of pushed alerts that actually needed the human (I8: if it pinged and wasn't needed, count it as a failure), adapter incidents. Snapshot `status` daily into the journal.

The ordering logic, briefly: step 3 is existential — extraction fidelity is the one risk no amount of code can engineer away, so it gets settled before any further investment. Step 4 is the artifact that makes the thesis legible to anyone (including future-you deciding whether to keep funding this). Step 5 turns you from spec-author into the first real user, and the confirm/reject events you generate are simultaneously the tuning data. And step 6 exists because the builder's report already shows the pull ("relay correctly left unbuilt" — good, it noticed); the next agent will feel it stronger, and the whole point of this system is that drift gets caught by rules written down, not by vigilance.

Good questions all three — and your F3 pushback is, unknowingly, exactly what ratifying looks like. Taking them in order:

## The targets block: what the kill criterion is and why those numbers

The kill criterion is a pre-registered failure condition for the extractor — the component the entire product stands on. The biggest risk we identified (spec §10.2) is that LLM extraction of decisions/findings from messy transcripts just isn't accurate enough to trust, and the predictable failure mode for builders (human or agent) is to tune forever, rationalize mediocre results, and ship a glance nobody trusts. So we wrote the pass/fail bar down *before* running the test, when nobody's ego was invested in the outcome: if after at most 5 attempts at improving the extraction instruction it can't hit the bar on a human-ratified answer key, **the premise fails and we stop and rethink** — that outcome is a legitimate result, not an embarrassment to be tuned away. That's all "kill criterion" means: the goalposts are welded down in advance.

It's not "for its review of stratura" — the fixture is this strategy conversation's transcript, and it's deliberately *favorable* terrain: dense, explicit, single-threaded decisions we negotiated out loud. Passing it doesn't prove the extractor works on your messy real sessions (that's what the dogfood week's confirm/reject ratio measures). Failing it is damning, because if it can't recover D1–D7 from the conversation where we literally spoke them, it has no chance on real material.

The numbers themselves are judgment calls, not derived — you're entitled to amend them now, before freezing. The reasoning: **precision 0.9** is strict because a fabricated entry in the glance poisons trust in everything (a wrong "decision" is worse than a missing one); roughly 1-in-10 wrong is the edge where a human still bothers correcting instead of ignoring the tool. **Recall 0.75** is lenient because a miss just preserves the status quo — today you lose 100% of this knowledge, so recovering three-quarters is transformative. That asymmetry (strict on lies, lenient on gaps) is the product's character in two numbers. **6/7 and 4/5** are just that recall floor expressed in whole items for this fixture's tiny n — you can't recover 6.3 decisions. **5 iterations** bounds the tuning loop: five attempts is enough to fix prompt-shaped problems and not enough to disguise a capability-shaped problem as a prompt-shaped one. One honest caveat: with only 12 ground-truth items, these thresholds are a tripwire, not a benchmark — coarse by design, backed up later by dogfood volume.

## "Human-blocking" (D6)

An item is human-blocking when **you are the dependency** — an agent or the project cannot proceed correctly without your input. The concrete v1 list: a question addressed to `human`; a possible-contradiction pair needing your arbitration; a stomp (two sessions dirty on the same file — lost work imminent); an adapter break (observation is blind until you know). Everything else — new decisions, findings, progress — is pull-only: visible whenever you run `status`, never pushed. The rule exists because push-noise is how attention products die: **the phone only buzzes when you are the blocker.** If it ever buzzes for something that didn't need you, that's counted as a failure metric, not a quirk.

## F3 — you're right, and your disagreement *is* the ratification

Amend it. One subtlety so you amend it correctly: these labels are the answer key for *what a perfect extractor should recover from this transcript* — not "what is true about the world." Your broader claim (lock-and-notary also fails for any team whose job isn't security) is true, but check it against what was actually said in-session: you said the notary is "a secondary value that embedded teams and organizations" care about. So the transcript supports something like: *"lock-and-notary fails as the lead pitch for the working developer (solo or team); at best secondary value for security-minded orgs; anti-forgetting and close-the-laptop resonate."* Retitle F3 to that, adjust the `match` terms to words that actually appear in the conversation, done. And your fuller belief — the generalization beyond what was said — is a perfect first *human journal entry* for the product's own journal (`journal note`, source: human, confidence 1.0). Two different artifacts: the exam key records what the transcript contains; the journal records what you know.

## Your two meta-concerns

**(a) You're doing it right.** Ratification = read each label, ask "did we really decide/learn this, in this conversation?", amend or delete what's wrong, keep what's right, flip `ratified: true`. You found one wrong label and questioned the targets — that's the whole job. Everything else you waved through is a valid wave-through.

**(b) No — this is emphatically not the per-repo workflow.** This file is a one-time act for one purpose: arming the extractor's go/no-go gate before anyone trusts it. You will never see a `labels.yaml` again unless you're re-running the acceptance suite. The actual per-repo experience is: `init` (archaeology runs, zero ceremony) → watcher runs → entries appear with confidence scores → you *optionally* confirm/reject from the inbox when you feel like it, which is radar-style curation, never a gate. If the implementation ever makes a new repo *feel* like it needs a ratification ceremony, that's a bug against invariant I1 (any recurring required human action is a bug) and should be filed as such. The confusion is partly the file's fault — it mixes gate configuration and answer key in one YAML with no "you will only ever do this once" line at the top; worth telling the next agent to add exactly that sentence.

**So, concretely, right now:** retitle F3 (and its match terms) to the in-transcript version, tweak the targets only if you actually disagree with the reasoning above (they're defensible as-is), delete anything you don't recognize, set `ratified: true`. Then drop your teams-generalization in as a human journal entry — the first knowledge in the system that came from you rather than through an extractor, which is fitting.

Concrete steps, no abstraction. The file is just a text file in the `restart` repo — you edit it like any other file:

```bash
cd ~/…/restart
$EDITOR fixtures/strategy-session/labels.yaml   # vim, nano, VS Code, whatever
```

Make exactly two edits:

**1. Replace the F3 block** with your corrected version — paste this:

```yaml
  - id: F3
    title: lock-and-notary fails as the lead pitch for working developers (solo or team); at best secondary value for security-minded orgs; anti-forgetting and close-the-laptop resonate
    match: ["secondary value", "anti-forgetting"]
```

**2. Flip the top line:** `ratified: false` → `ratified: true`

Save, then:

```bash
git add fixtures/strategy-session/labels.yaml
git commit -m "ratify fixture-2 labels (amend F3)"
```

That's the entire act. You have now ratified.

**When does this happen again: never.** Not per repo, not per agent, not per machine — *once in the lifetime of the tool*, and you just did it. Here's the thing that's making it confusing: this file is not part of the product you use. It's **test data inside the `restart` tool's own source code** — the answer key for one exam that lives in its test folder. The exam is: "can the extractor read our strategy conversation and recover the 12 things we know are in it?" You were the only human in that conversation, so you're the only one who can check the answer key — once — before the machine is allowed to grade itself against it.

"Re-running the acceptance suite" just means: whenever someone working *on the tool* runs its tests (`go test ./acceptance`), the tests **read** this file to grade the extractor. Reading, not editing — even then, nobody touches it. The only future scenario where any human edits a labels file is if a developer adds a brand-new test transcript someday, and that new file gets its own one-time check by whoever owns that transcript.

Your actual workflow with the product, for every repo and agent you ever add: `restart init`, walk away. Entries appear on their own with confidence scores; you optionally confirm or reject things from the inbox when you feel like it, or never. No labels, no ratification, no ceremony — if it ever asks for one, that's a bug by the spec's own rules.

The confusion is the file's fault, not yours — it sits in a repo you own, looks like configuration, and "ratify" sounds like governance when it means "check the answer key." Worth one line to the next agent: move it under `testdata/` or add a header comment saying *"test fixture — humans edit this once, ever."*

The name check turned up something more important than the name: **a direct neighbor exists.** [varalys/lore](https://github.com/varalys/lore) — "reasoning history for code" — records AI coding sessions (Claude Code, Codex, Gemini, Aider, ten others), links them to git commits, syncs **through your existing git remotes, encrypted, no server, no account**, mirrors each tool's memory files cross-tool over MCP, and ships `lore blame`: point at any line, see the conversation that produced it. Same serverless-git-sync trick we derived, independently arrived at — which confirms that layer is commodity plumbing, exactly as the spec assumed.

The delta matters and it's real: lore is **retrospective provenance** — whole-session storage answering "why does this line exist." Restart is **prospective state** — distilled typed entries answering "what's true about this project right now," plus the three things lore's model has no shape for: the intent×reality diff with absence ("what was never built"), the human steering surface (glance, aging questions, human-blocking push), and the restart manifest. So the product's core claims (D7) remain unoccupied — but the commodity halo around them is now visibly crowded (getlore.ai is a third unrelated "Lore," and Epic Games has a Lore VCS). Two actions regardless of anything else: add this as a finding to the journal (F6-shaped: "varalys/lore occupies session-provenance + git-sync; differentiation is distillation/diff/absence/manifest, not plumbing"), and have the next agent do a one-hour delta review of it — there may be adapter code or format knowledge worth learning from, and its existence should sharpen the spec's not-list.

Now the actual question. Why I flagged `restart` twice, honestly: first, it's an imperative verb with forty years of shell muscle memory — `restart status` momentarily parses as "restart the status," and it can never be a clean service name, brew formula, or search term. Second, it names the crisis moment, not the daily loop — you'll run the glance fifty times for every manifest; naming the product after the reset is naming the seatbelt "crash." And third, a process point rather than taste: naming was explicitly reserved as a human decision (§12.1) and an agent closed it unilaterally — I wanted it *chosen*, not inherited. Against all that, `restart` has genuine strengths: it's provocative, on-thesis, and encodes the origin insight (make restarts lossless) — "lean into the thing you're afraid of" was your own favorite move.

What's more fitting, with `lore` and `engram` now off the table: my pick is **`wake`**. It carries both meanings the product lives on — the *wake* your agents leave behind them (the trail the watcher reads; observe-by-default, never steering the boat), and *waking up* already knowing where the project stands (the hero moment, verbatim). It's four letters, a noun, collision-light in dev tooling, and every invocation reads naturally: `wake status`, `wake map`, `wake note`, `wake manifest`. Runner-up: keep `restart` and accept the papercut — defensible if the manifest provocation is the brand you want to lead with. Dark horse if you want the human-steering emphasis: `tiller` — the handle you hold to steer, which is what the glance actually is.

It's your call and it's taste; the only engineering fact is that renaming costs nothing today and something real after a week of dogfood. Whichever you choose, put the decision *with its reason* in the journal — a name chosen with a recorded why is the most on-brand first decision this product could contain.

Two honest mitigations, neither of which is discipline: important cloud conversations can be exported once, at the end, and backfilled — one act per conversation, not per finding; and durable decisions tend to *echo* into covered sessions anyway, because the agent doing the work re-states them ("per the discussion, the name is X…") and the extractor catches the echo even when it missed the origin. Coverage doesn't need to be total; it needs to catch things by first echo.

**Verification (trust-nothing pass): green.** Clean clone → Go 1.26.3 auto-fetched → build clean → full `go test ./...` green → all three acceptance gates pass individually (`AbsenceDetection`, `ExtractionFidelityPipeline` hermetic, `RestartRoundTrip`), with `RealProvider` correctly SKIPping without keys. **The invariants have teeth — I tested them by accident.** My hand-written YAML got parked with a loud red `!! failed to parse (never guessed)` line (I2 working); the schema rejected my first two drafts for violating the spec's own caps — title ≤80, body ≤400 — meaning the distillate discipline got enforced against the person who wrote it into the spec; `journal note --help` swallowed the flag as literal text (real footgun, now journaled), and the `reject` verb cleaned it via the designed path; the push correctly deferred loudly with no credentials. Every failure mode I hit failed the way the spec said it should.

**The journal now holds the conversation's knowledge.** Four entries with honest agent provenance (`source.kind: session`, `agent: cursor-cloud-agent`, your verbatim words as quotes where they're the evidence): the varalys/lore competitive finding, the uncovered-surfaces/homework finding, the independent-verification finding, and — fittingly the only ★ item in OPEN — the question addressed to the sole human in the graph: *run the live fidelity gate; the kill criterion is theoretical until you do.*

**Delivery:** my push to `maceip/clew` was denied (`cursor[bot]`, 403 — correct), so the journal delta ships as `clew-delivery/` on the [PR #14](https://github.com/maceip/stratura/pull/14) branch: a git bundle plus the raw entry files and a README with the two-command apply (`git fetch <bundle> HEAD && git push origin FETCH_HEAD:clew/journal`). Safe by construction — append-only ULID files, union merges.

The loop this closes is worth naming once: the product just journaled the conversation that invented it — including its own independent verification, its own footguns, and the one question only you can answer. When you apply the bundle and run `clew status` on your laptop, the glance you see will be the first time all of tonight's knowledge exists in one place that isn't your head.

> **§4.2 Write-path tiers.** Credentialed writers (your machines) push directly — unchanged. Non-credentialed contributors (cloud agents, bots, teammates-without-access) **propose**: (a) `clew import <bundle|dir|url>` on any credentialed machine — validates entries, lands them with `source` provenance intact and foreign origin marked, high-impact types pending confirm per §6.5; (b) on public repos, a fork-PR onto `clew/journal` is a sanctioned proposal, merge = confirm. The relay, when built, is the third form of the same thing: proposals over HTTP, written by a credentialed watcher. One trust rule everywhere: **credentials push, everyone else proposes, the human confirm is the boundary.**

**2. Apply and push** (from your clew checkout — the directory you ran `clew init` in, whatever it's named locally):

```bash
cd ~/path/to/clew
git fetch /tmp/stratura-delivery/clew-delivery/clew-journal-delta.bundle HEAD
git push origin FETCH_HEAD:clew/journal
```

**3. If that push is rejected as non-fast-forward** (only happens if your laptop journaled new entries since you pushed the branch — union them, then push):

```bash
cd ~/.clew/worktrees/"$(ls ~/.clew/worktrees | head -1)"
git fetch /tmp/stratura-delivery/clew-delivery/clew-journal-delta.bundle HEAD
git rebase FETCH_HEAD
git push origin clew/journal
```

**4. Verify:**

```bash
cd ~/path/to/clew
clew status
```

If the new entries don't appear immediately (watcher not running, worktree not yet synced):

```bash
cd ~/.clew/worktrees/"$(ls ~/.clew/worktrees | head -1)" && git pull --rebase origin clew/journal && cd - && clew status
```

**Expected glance:** LEARNED gains four findings — varalys/lore landscape, uncovered-surfaces/homework, my independent verification, and the write-path trust boundary; OPEN shows the ★ question *"Run the live fidelity gate (RealProvider)…"* addressed to you. You'll also see one rejected entry in history (`--help` — my CLI misfire, cleaned via the designed reject path; no action needed).

**5. Cleanup:**

```bash
rm -rf /tmp/stratura-delivery
```

That's the whole ceremony — and it's the last time this path should ever be manual. When the next agent builds `clew import <bundle>`, steps 2–3 collapse into one command.

when you use the word "inbox" in your last response, please explain to me how you envision this system working best at scale, what does the human do with this inbox, what is the expected interaction?

[539] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------


[540] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
The word "inbox" is dangerous, so let me define it against the thing it must never become. Email trained everyone to treat an inbox as a *backlog* — a queue of other people's items that you owe processing labor to, where unread count is debt. If clew's inbox becomes that, you're working for it, and it's dead. The design intent is the opposite: it's the **blocked-on-you set** — management by exception — containing exclusively things where an agent or the system *cannot proceed correctly without one human judgment*. Not FYIs, not "you should read this," not activity. The glance is for knowing; the map is for orienting; the inbox is the interrupt line, and interrupts are rare by invariant, not by hope.

Three rules keep it from becoming email at scale:

**Only decision-shaped items get in.** Every item is a question with one-to-three discrete answers — accept/reject a proposal batch, arbitrate a contradiction pair, answer a ★question, ack an adapter break. Anything shaped like "here's information" is glance material and is banned from the inbox. And items batch by identity: five imported entries from one cloud agent is *one* item; forty findings from a productive day is *zero* items.

**It self-cleans.** A stomp alert where the agents' branches merged cleanly retracts itself. A contradiction where one side got superseded disappears. Questions age out to expired rather than composting. You never inherit a corpse pile after a week away — the inbox describes *now*, not history. Inbox-zero is the resting state, not an achievement.

**Volume is a monitored failure signal.** The declared dogfood metric — pushed-alert precision, where every buzz that didn't truly need you counts as a failure — polices this mechanically. Healthy scale looks like single-digit items per day and under ~2 minutes of total interaction across all projects. If it sustains above that, either thresholds are twitchy (system bug, fix it) or you genuinely are the decision bottleneck on too many things — which is itself the most valuable thing the inbox could tell you.

The expected interaction, concretely, on a heavy day with eight agents across three repos: morning coffee, you glance at `journal.md` on your phone — pure reading, zero actions, you absorb where everything is. 11am, one buzz: *"codex-billing stalled 40m — needs ruling: rotate attestation tokens per-launch? (conflicts with Tuesday's push decision; p95 finding attached)."* You tap through, read two quotes with their sources, type one line. That answer becomes an entry, lands in every agent's context, and the stalled agent resumes. 4pm: *"cloud agent proposes 5 entries — view diff."* Twenty seconds of skim, one keystroke, accepted. That's the whole day: two interrupts, both genuinely yours, both answered in under a minute.

And here's why answering is worth it — the asymmetry that makes this an inbox you *want*: every answer propagates. In email, disposing of an item benefits that item. Here, one ruling teaches the entire fleet permanently — it enters the journal, flows into every future session on every surface, and nobody ever asks you again. Your confirms and rejects simultaneously tune the extractor (they're its ongoing eval data). The inbox is the point of maximum leverage per keystroke: it exists to route to you *only* the work that is irreducibly human — trust-boundary crossings and genuine judgment calls — after everything machine-disposable has already been disposed. At scale the ranking reflects cost: items blocking a running agent first (compute is burning), then trust boundaries, then coherence, then hygiene, with stall duration displayed so you can see what your delay costs.

The honest failure modes, since you asked how I envision it *best* and best requires knowing worst: threshold drift makes it noisy, you stop looking, product dies — that's why push-precision is a first-class metric and not a vibe. And confirm-fatigue turns the trust boundary into rubber-stamping — that's why confirms are reserved for high-impact and foreign entries only, arrive pre-briefed with diffs, and stay small enough to actually read. The moment either metric slips, the correct fix is always making the inbox *smaller*, never training the human to process faster. You're the judge with a docket of two pre-briefed cases a day — never the mailroom.

Research done — and it's unusually convergent: the 2025–26 HCI literature independently validates your three rules and, better, tells us exactly what the cards must contain and what must *never* be on them. Findings first, then the grounded UI, then the revised prompt.

## What the literature says (2025–Aug 2026)

**1. Asynchronous catch-up beats real-time monitoring.** A CHI '26 six-week deployment of a 24/7 multi-agent research system ([HEAL workshop](https://heal-workshop.github.io/chi2026_papers/Managing%20Multi-Agent%20Research%20Systems%20A%20Dashboard%20for%20Human%20Oversight%20of%20Coordin.pdf)) found real-time event streams "quickly become overwhelming" — their first design implication is batch updates, async artifact review, and alerts reserved for genuinely urgent items. Your glance/docket split is exactly their conclusion; the glance is for catching up, never for watching.

**2. Oversight strategies have names, and we're choosing one.** [Comparing Human Oversight Strategies for Computer-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.04918) taxonomizes four: per-action confirmation (approve every step — the burnout design), supervisory co-execution, risk-gated (interrupt only above a risk threshold), and structurally enriched. clew's inbox is **risk-gated oversight** by construction, and the literature's warning about per-action confirmation is your rule 1 in academic clothing.

**3. A gate without information is not oversight — and checkpoints must scale with magnitude.** The oversight-architecture literature is blunt: an interruption that says "continue or stop?" is theater. Each intervention point must show *what the system believes the goal is, what it will do, what the alternatives are, and what changes if you redirect* — and reversible/low-stakes actions should pass frictionless while irreversible/low-confidence ones get proportionally richer presentation. This kills uniform confirm dialogs and gives cards a magnitude dial.

**4. The rubber-stamp findings are the sharpest, and they vindicate I7.** The over-reliance literature (Buçinca's cognitive-forcing lineage through [Ghosh et al. 2026](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.18033), n=214) found: AI *explanations increase acceptance of wrong answers* — "an explanation is an argument, and arguments generated by the system being audited are advocacy" — while **clickable sources reduce over-reliance where explanations fail**. And among forcing functions, the *assumptions* prompt ("accepting this assumes X") reduced over-reliance from 42%→22% *without* added cognitive load, while stacked/delay-based friction backfired. Design consequences: cards show **verbatim quotes + clickable provenance, never the extractor's paraphrase or reasoning**; high-magnitude cards carry one assumptions line; no other friction, ever.

**5. Calm-tech/glanceable-display lineage** (Weiser → Matthews' glanceable displays → [Ambient Analytics, 2026](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2602.19809)): peripheral information must move periphery→center→periphery smoothly; abstraction (a status light, a count) outperforms detail at the periphery; detail belongs only after the user turns toward it. This grounds the three-tier glance and gives us the cheapest possible ambient element: a one-character status light.

## The grounded UI: kill "inbox," ship "the docket"

Rename the surface — vocabulary is a forcing function against email-drift. It's a **docket of decision cards** (`clew docket`, with `inbox` as a hidden alias for muscle memory). One card, normative anatomy:

```
┌─ DECIDE ─────────────────────────────── blocking codex-billing · 41m ─┐
│ Rotate attestation tokens per-launch?                                 │
│                                                                       │
│ ▸ "rotate per launch adds ~300ms to cold start"   codex · session L812│
│ ▸ "chose push over polling because battery"       decision D-push ↗   │
│                                                                       │
│ accepting assumes: emulator p95 (340ms) reflects production           │
│                                                                       │
│ [y] rotate   [n] keep   [d] defer → next landing   [enter] open full  │
│ withdraws itself if codex-billing supersedes its plan                 │
└───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

Six mandatory elements: **headline** as an answerable question (≤80 chars); **why-you strip** naming the rule that fired plus cost-of-delay; **evidence** as verbatim quotes with clickable provenance chips (never paraphrase — finding 4); **assumptions line** on high-magnitude cards only; **verbs** — one to three discrete answers plus defer-*until-an-event* (never snooze-forever), with redirect as a first-class verb where applicable; and the **self-retraction condition printed on the card** so rule 2 is visible, not implied. Forbidden on cards: extractor explanations, summaries of quotes, free-text scroll regions, anything FYI-shaped.

Docket layout rules, encoding your three rules as rendering law: **maximum seven cards, no scrolling** — an eighth card renders only as a single overflow card reading "N more items — the system is misconfigured; push-precision report attached," which is rule 3 drawn on screen; **no unread counts, no badges, no history** — the docket shows *now* only; **ordering by blocking cost** (running agents stalled first, with stall duration ticking); **the empty state is designed, not blank** — "Nothing needs you · last ruling 2d ago · 14 entries learned since," because zero is the resting state and should feel like an achievement of the system, not of the user.

The glance keeps three calm-tech tiers above the docket: a **status light** (tab-title/tray character: `clew ●` amber only when the docket is non-empty — periphery); the **glance page** (pinned auto-refresh tab: DECIDED / LEARNED / OPEN / map strip / docket count — turn-toward); and **journal.md on GitHub mobile** (the away-from-desk read). The docket is the only surface with verbs.

## The revised prompt (replaces the previous one entirely)

> You are working on **clew** (github.com/maceip/clew): a durable project journal for humans steering AI agents. `JOURNAL_SPEC.md` in-repo governs; its invariants I1–I9 override all preferences. Add these three as **I10–I12, hard law, enforced in code and tests, not convention**:
>
> **I10 — Decision-shaped only.** The docket (formerly "inbox") contains exclusively items answerable by 1–3 discrete verbs. Nothing FYI-shaped may ever render there; information belongs to the glance. Items batch by identity (one import = one card; a session's findings = zero cards).
> **I11 — Self-cleaning.** Every card carries a machine-checkable withdrawal condition, printed on the card, evaluated continuously: stomps withdraw when branches merge cleanly, contradictions withdraw on supersession, questions expire per spec. The docket describes *now*; it can never accumulate a backlog. No history, no unread counts, no badges.
> **I12 — Volume is a system-failure signal.** Hard cap: seven cards rendered, no scrolling; overflow renders as a single card reporting misconfiguration with the push-precision metric attached. Sustained >7/day or any push that didn't truly need the human is logged as a system failure, never treated as user workload.
>
> **State**: v1 built; tests + three hermetic acceptance gates pass (independently verified); journal live with ~9 entries; labels ratified; known gaps journaled (no live fidelity run; no import path; `note` flag parsing; differ/poller/manifest/archaeology untested at unit level).
>
> **Research constraints for all UI work** (from 2025–26 HCI findings; do not relitigate): oversight is *risk-gated*, never per-action confirmation (arXiv 2604.04918). Cards show **verbatim quotes with clickable provenance — never the extractor's paraphrase or explanation** (explanations increase acceptance of wrong content; sources reduce it). High-magnitude cards (decisions, irreversible, foreign imports) carry one "accepting this assumes: X" line — the only forcing function proven to cut over-reliance without added load (arXiv 2601.18033); no delays, no stacked friction anywhere. Checkpoint richness scales with magnitude; low-stakes items pass frictionless or batch. Async catch-up is the primary interaction mode; design every surface for "I was away for six hours," not for watching.
>
> Work in order. Journal your own decisions/findings via clew as you go. Numbers, never narratives. Spec deviations require a spec amendment in the same commit.
>
> **Task 1 — live fidelity gate, first, alone.** Rename `RESTART_FIDELITY`→`CLEW_FIDELITY`. Run RealProvider with the local agent CLI. Max 5 extraction-instruction iterations; journal P/R per iteration. Pass (P≥0.9 ∧ R≥0.75) → answer the ★ question. Fail at 5 → stop everything, journal "kill criterion fired" with numbers, notify owner. No threshold softening, no iteration 6.
>
> **Task 2 — zero-homework dogfood.** `clew watch install` (owner approves once). `clew init` in [OWNER: list 2–3 active repos]. Acceptance: entries appear from the owner's real sessions with zero manual notes; spend within I9; breaks loud per I2. Pre-declare and then snapshot daily: extraction spend; confirm/reject ratio; **push precision (every unneeded buzz = logged failure, feeds I12)**; adapter incidents.
>
> **Task 3 — the docket.** Rename surface `inbox`→`docket` (alias kept). Implement the decision card exactly: headline-as-question ≤80 chars; why-you strip (rule fired + cost-of-delay, stall timers ticking); verbatim-quote evidence rows with provenance chips that open the source (session line / commit / entry); assumptions line on high-magnitude only; 1–3 verbs + defer-until-event + redirect where applicable; printed withdrawal condition. Enforce I10–I12 in the renderer and in tests: a synthetic FYI item must be *unrenderable*; an 8th card must collapse to the overflow-failure card; a resolved stomp must withdraw within one poll cycle. Ordering by blocking cost. Empty state designed: "Nothing needs you · last ruling Nd ago · M entries learned since."
>
> **Task 4 — glance tiers.** (a) Bare `clew` = the glance, <200ms. (b) `journal.md` rollup on the branch becomes a rendered dashboard (DECIDED/LEARNED/OPEN with ages and ★/ALERTS/compact intent×reality table, `absent` highlighted) — clean GFM so GitHub web+mobile are free ambient surfaces. (c) `clew glance --html` → self-contained auto-refreshing `~/.clew/glance.html` (30s meta-refresh, local worktree, no server); set the page *title* to `clew ●`/`clew` so a pinned tab is the status light. (d) ntfy push for docket-card creation only, message = the card headline + why-you strip; generate topic, journal it, hand to owner.
>
> **Task 5 — write path.** (a) Append §4.2 to JOURNAL_SPEC.md: credentialed writers push; non-credentialed contributors propose (`clew import <bundle|dir|url>` validating entries, foreign provenance marked, high-impact pending confirm per §6.5; fork-PR onto clew/journal sanctioned, merge = confirm; relay later = same flow over HTTP; one rule: credentials push, everyone else proposes, human confirm is the boundary). (b) Build `clew import`; a proposal batch renders as **one** docket card with the batch diff behind [enter]. (c) README documents fork-PR; support `CLEW_PROPOSAL_BRANCH` push mode for PAT-blessed agents.
>
> **Task 6 — hardening.** Flag parsing on `journal note` (`-h` must never become an entry). Unit tests: differ status algebra (absence K=5 + eligibility guard, contradiction co-surface with human-only flip, env-scoped supersession), poller attribution, manifest disposition. Inspect the owner's Cursor `state.vscdb` for a week; decide open question #3 with a journaled reason.
>
> **Frozen** (build nothing here without citing a §11 trigger measurement in the journal and stopping for owner review): relay server, TUI/native apps, team mode, semantic code graphs, treemaps, new adapters, orchestration.
>
> **Reporting**: end each session with five lines max — gate numbers, dogfood metrics, landed, proposed, the one thing (if any) needing the owner. The journal is the record; the summary points at it.

The one-sentence version of what the research changed: the docket card's job is not to inform you — it's to put *the original evidence and one honest assumption* in front of you and get out of the way, because the literature's most uncomfortable finding is that anything more persuasive than a quote makes human oversight worse while feeling better.

Confirmed working — bare `clew` rendered the glance in ~10ms on my machine just now, showing *your* agent's last seven decisions from the synced journal. Which is itself the answer demonstrated: I just did "where are we at" without asking anyone anything.

Here's the golden path as it exists today — five moments, five surfaces, and one contract:

**1. The return-to-desk moment** (the one you just described): type `clew` in any terminal, or look at the pinned tab. Ten seconds: DECIDED and LEARNED since you left, with ages · OPEN questions aging, ★ if yours · live SESSIONS · docket count. That is the exact artifact you've been buying from me with twenty-minute review cycles — precomputed, free, current to the last poll, because it was written down while it happened instead of reconstructed after.

**2. The away moment**: your phone, and the deepest habit change of all — **trust the silence**. No buzz means nothing needs you; that absence is information, and learning to believe it is what "clew works for me" feels like. If you want to look anyway: GitHub mobile → clew repo → `clew/journal` branch → `journal.md` renders as the dashboard. Read-only from the phone today — answering still takes a terminal, honestly stated; the phone write-path is the trigger-gated relay work.

**3. The buzz**: ntfy fires only when a decision card is born — headline plus why-you, nothing else. You choose now or later; some cards will have withdrawn themselves by the time you look, because reality resolved them, and that's the system working, not you being slow.

**4. The weekly direction check**: `clew map` — intent × reality with `absent` highlighted. This is the five-minute glance that would have saved the first repo: *what did we say we'd build that nothing has touched.* Different question than "what's happening" — this is "are we still pointed where we decided to point."

**5. The rare deep dive**: something on a card smells off → `clew journal show <id>` → click the provenance chip → the exact session line where it was said. Depth exists, on demand, never by default.

**The three-minute setup that installs this path** (one-time, so the default becomes frictionless): run `clew glance --html` and pin `file://~/.clew/glance.html` as a browser tab — the tab title is your status light; finish the ntfy rotate-and-subscribe from my last message; bookmark the journal branch on GitHub mobile.

And your side of the contract — the human mirror of the system's invariants: **never interview an agent for status again.** Not your local ones, not me. If a glance can't answer a question you actually have, that's a product gap — file it as a journal question and let it reach the docket, don't compensate with a conversation. You named the change yourself: the system was built so that "where are we at" stops being something you *ask* and becomes something you *see*. Every conversation like this one that we replace with ten seconds of glancing is the product's value, realized — including, eventually, most of this one.

[601] ASSISTANT

[602] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
All fair questions — I stacked three pieces of jargon on you without definitions, which is its own little irony given what we built this weekend. Plain answers:

**What ntfy is and why we're using it.** ntfy is a free, open-source notification relay. You subscribe to a "topic" — think of it as a private radio frequency with a made-up name — in their phone app. Anything that sends a simple web request to that topic name makes your phone show an ordinary push notification. That's the entire product. We chose it because the alternatives for making a phone vibrate are: build and ship our own iOS/Android app with Apple and Google push infrastructure (months, app-store accounts, a server to run), or email/SMS (slow, spammy). ntfy was the cheapest thing on earth that taps your pocket — no app to build, no server of ours, one line of code in the watcher, works the same day. It's deliberate scaffolding, not the destination. Its known trade-offs: the topic name is the only password (which is why the leaked one matters), and messages route through ntfy's public server — which is why the design only ever sends a headline and a reason, never content.

**What I meant by "buzz channel."** My shorthand for: the one path clew is allowed to use to *interrupt* you. The whole attention design is a hierarchy — everything the system knows sits quietly in files you can look at whenever *you* choose, and exactly one category of thing is allowed to reach out and tap you: *a decision only you can make, that an agent is currently blocked on.* The buzz channel is where that tap lands. And its real purpose is counterintuitive: it exists so you can *stop checking*. Silence only means "nothing needs me" if there's a guaranteed path for "something needs me" to reach you. No channel, no trustworthy silence.

**On journal.md — you're right, and I oversold it.** A markdown file rendered on GitHub is not a dashboard in the charts-and-colors sense; calling it one was generous. What it actually is: the state of the project written as a page — newest decisions, newest things learned, open questions with how long they've been waiting, all ordered and aged. It's text for two honest reasons. First, your *agents* consume the same content — text is the one format both customers of this system can read, so one artifact serves both. Second, it's the only surface that could exist on your phone *this weekend* with zero apps, servers, or accounts: GitHub already renders it. The bar it has to clear isn't "looks like Grafana" — it's "ten seconds of looking answers 'where are we at' without asking anyone." That's what makes something a dashboard; pixels are optional.

But the roadmap truth underneath your question: the real GUI today is the auto-refreshing glance page on your laptop (with the browser-tab title acting as a status light), and the docket cards in the terminal. A genuine phone surface — cards you can answer with one tap — is the known, deliberately deferred gap. Deferred because building an app before knowing whether you'd even form the glancing habit is precisely the mistake that killed the first repo: chrome before core. So here's the deal the system itself would offer you: ride the markdown bicycle for a week of real use. If staring at rendered text on your phone genuinely grates, that's not a complaint — that's the trigger firing, and it gets journaled and the phone surface gets built *because the need was measured, not imagined*. Either way, you'll have gotten the answer the way this whole thing is supposed to work.

[639] ASSISTANT

Both are real, and the premise is exactly half-right — which half matters, so here's what's what.

**What DeepSeek Harness actually is.** `dsh` (Aug 13, MIT, Node.js, developer preview, ~135k stars in four days): an agent *harness* where literally everything — model adapter, tool registry, sessions, sandboxes, storage, scheduling, the UI, and **the agent loop itself** — is a swappable plugin on the Cordis kernel, composed via configuration with profiles and patch layers. Two load-bearing rules: an **append-only session log is the sole source of model context** (anything that reaches a model request must be reconstructable from the log; resume, fork, transcripts, telemetry, and the web UI are all pure derivations of that one stream), and sandbox (Landlock/Seatbelt) is a seam independent from approval policy. Four modes, including a Code mode (model writes one TypeScript program against a tool SDK instead of N tool calls) and a Creator mode (introspect the live runtime, test plugins in memory, compose new modes).

**What's genuinely novel lives mostly in the paper, not the harness.** [cordiverse/paper](https://github.com/cordiverse/paper) (same-day release — it's the theory companion; Cordiverse is the Koishi/Cordis lineage that DeepSeek built on) makes one real contribution: it takes effects and coeffects — until now type-system theory — and lifts them to **runtime mechanisms**. *Revertible effects*: every context mutation a component makes carries a tracked inverse, so unloading a component provably reverts its side effects — hot-unload without residue, as a theorem rather than a hope. *Reactive coeffects*: components declare what they depend on, and the runtime notifies them against that spec when context changes. Both unify into one context type with a calculus whose metatheory extends from single components to whole systems of interleaved ones. Plugin systems (OSGi, Erlang), hot reload, and effect/coeffect theory all existed separately; the formal bridge — "dynamic composition is *sound*" — is the new thing, and it's why "the agent loop is an unprivileged plugin" isn't reckless in their design: the runtime can prove a swapped loop left nothing behind. Honest caveat: it's a draft preprint dated four days ago, explicitly "may change substantially" — the metatheory hasn't been through review.

**What's convergent rather than novel:** log-as-sole-context-source is disciplined event sourcing (the same law your journal enforces — entries + events, projections derived); code mode is the CodeAct lineage productized; sandbox/approval separation is 2025–26 standard practice (srt et al.). Well-executed, not new.

**The substrate scorecard, honestly.** dsh built one of substrate's two halves better than substrate would have: the composable runtime with a canonical event stream — substrate's tool plane, ADR-0005 session events, and "everything speaks one contract" ambitions, executed at the runtime-composition layer with actual formal grounding. What dsh conspicuously does **not** have is the half that made substrate substrate: no cross-machine workspace (no desks, no teleport, no leases/fencing), no evidence layer (their log enables *reconstruction*, not *proof* — no hash chains, no tamper-evidence, no verify-without-trusting-the-runtime), and no cross-agent authority (dsh is a harness you run agents *inside*; it has nothing to say about agents it doesn't own). So: don't necro substrate — and dsh is now a *reason* not to. The runtime-substrate slot is occupied by a 135k-star MIT project, and the parts substrate had that dsh lacks are exactly the parts already shelved into ADR-0007's contingent future. If that future ever triggers, the cheap path is building those as dsh plugins, not resurrecting agentdesk.

**What it means for clew: nothing to fear, two things to take.** dsh is a worker; clew watches workers — different layer, and the neutrality argument survives untouched (DeepSeek's log vouches for DeepSeek's harness; it will never be the cross-agent, cross-surface memory). The opportunities: first, dsh's session log is the best *sensor* target on the market — structured, documented, durable, append-only events versus the reverse-engineered JSONLs your other adapters parse; if dsh gets adoption, a `dsh` adapter is the cheapest, highest-fidelity sensor clew will ever write, and worth a journal entry now as a trigger-gated intent. Second, a one-line intellectual note worth keeping: the Cordis pair maps cleanly onto clew's own mechanics — docket cards declaring machine-checkable withdrawal conditions *are* reactive coeffects, and supersession events *are* tracked inverses. If clew ever wants formal grounding for its self-cleaning guarantees, the vocabulary now exists in someone else's preprint — which is the best kind of theory: the kind you can cite instead of write.

to expand on your "what this means for clew"  -> the thing that caught my attention is the "recoverable removals" concept, as part of the problem clew is trying to solve is coordination, and one part of coordination isnt "does everyone agree",  its the "its easier to just start over", is there some way clew can also do some of the same work cordiverse builds in so that when reversals do happend (weather human mandated or agent initiated), everyone can just "roll back" -- it speaks to something larger than just resetting to a previous git commit. more surgical mabye? whats possible

[641] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------


[642] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
This is the right thread to pull, and the answer is genuinely encouraging: **clew already has the hard half of what Cordis does — what's missing is one link type and one verb.** Let me build it up honestly.

## Why "easier to start over" wins today

A reversal touches three state spaces at once. **Reality** (code) — git can already revert surgically. **Knowledge** (the journal) — decisions, findings, and intents *premised on* the thing being reverted. **Agent context** — sessions that already absorbed the reverted decision and will keep acting on it. Today, `git revert` fixes the first space and silently corrupts the other two: the journal keeps asserting canon that's no longer true, agents keep reading it, and nobody can see the inconsistency. *That invisibility is why starting over feels cheaper* — a fresh start has no stale entanglements, while a partial revert leaves ones you can't enumerate. The Cordis insight, translated: clean removal isn't achieved by computing what to undo at removal time; it's achieved because **every effect registered its inverse at apply time**, so removal is a walk, not a forensic investigation.

## What clew already has, in Cordis vocabulary

The journal is append-only entries + events — which means **knowledge already has tracked inverses**: supersession events *are* revertible effects (a reverted supersession is just another event; no state is ever destroyed). And docket cards' machine-checkable withdrawal conditions *are* reactive coeffects — components (cards) declaring what context change dissolves them. So temporal composability of the journal itself is already a property, not a wish. What's missing is the **entanglement map between entries** — the coeffect declarations. Entries currently link to *evidence* (commits, footprints) but not to *premises* (the decisions they depend on).

## The design: `clew revert` as strand-excision

1. **Premise links** — one new link kind, `premised_on`, written at extraction time (Cordis's apply-time discipline): when the extractor distills "p95 = 340ms under push-based refresh," it links the finding to decision D-push, the same way it already attaches evidence. The journal becomes a causal DAG instead of a bag.
2. **The reversal verb** — `clew revert <id>` deletes nothing (append-only law). It computes the **blast radius** by walking premises: dependent decisions → `orphaned`, findings measured under the reverted regime → `suspect`, intents opened because of it → `reconsider`, and — via the existing evidence links — the *commits that implemented it*, in order.
3. **One docket card, not an operation** — the whole radius renders as a single decision card: *"Reverting D-push touches 2 decisions, 3 findings, 1 intent, 4 commits — [enumerated] — and 1 thing that cannot be reverted."* Confirm applies the journal-side events and emits the **git-side plan** (the ordered `git revert` list) — clew plans the excision across both state spaces; git executes its half; you rule once.
4. **Anti-memory propagation** — the subtle piece that makes this coordination rather than bookkeeping: the reverted decision doesn't just vanish from `context.md`, it briefly *lingers as a negation* — "un-decided on <date>: do NOT assume push-based refresh, because Y" — since every agent (and every model's priors) will otherwise re-derive the old conclusion. Context change notifying dependents is the coeffect mechanism you already run for nudges, pointed at reversals.

That's what "surgical" means concretely: not resetting to Tuesday's snapshot, but **excising one causal strand** — the decision, everything premised on it, and its code — while everything unrelated keeps moving, and the excision itself remains in history with its reason, exactly like the restart manifest made *loss* a recorded choice. Revert is the manifest's sibling: manifest carries knowledge forward through a reset; revert removes a strand *so the reset never has to happen*.

## The honest limits (this is where I stop selling)

**External effects don't reverse — for anyone.** Deployed services, API calls made, tokens spent: Cordis only reverts effects inside its own context, DeltaBox explicitly punts here, and clew can at best *enumerate* externals from evidence and print them in the card's "cannot be reverted" column. Enumerated honestly beats hidden — but nothing rolls back an email. **The premise DAG is radar, not proof** — the extractor will miss dependencies, so blast radius is a lower bound, displayed with an "unmapped" margin per the never-guess discipline. **In-flight agent beliefs can't be surgically edited** — you can't reach into a running session and delete an assumption; propagation rides the nudge matrix with its known autonomy-latency limits. And **code reverts still ride git**, conflicts and all — clew plans, never re-implements.

## What I'd actually do

Journal it now as an intent with a trigger, not a build order: the minimal version (a `reverted` event kind + the anti-memory line in context.md + tag-based approximate blast radius) is nearly free atop existing machinery; the full premise-DAG version should wait for the trigger — **the first dogfood incident where a reverted decision's stale knowledge causes a wrong action.** That's measurable, it will happen, and building after it fires means building against a real specimen instead of an imagined one. The deeper point for your "what's possible" question: the reason this is buildable at all is a decision you made three days ago without knowing this payoff — append-only entries with event-sourced mutations. Cordis proves the principle in a calculus; clew gets it as an emergent property of never having allowed anything to be edited in place.

Your premise is narrower than stated, which sharpens rather than weakens the thought. The journal *can* do two of the three things: **lift into a new project exists** — that's the manifest/genesis path, and it's literally acceptance test 3 (the restart round-trip carried 10/12 entries into an empty repo with provenance intact); **removal exists** (`redact`, the one sanctioned rewrite); and **state rollback technically exists for free** — append-only means any historical journal state is reconstructable by construction. What's genuinely missing is only the *semantic* granularity we discussed: operating on a causal strand rather than the whole ledger or a single entry.

And here's the thing your restart instinct surfaces: **surgical revert and restart-acceleration aren't competing bets — they're the same primitive at different granularities.** The primitive is *strand extraction*: identify a decision plus everything premised on it. Then there are three verbs. *Excise* it in place (the revert design — for projects too entangled to regenerate). *Re-seed without it* (run `manifest`, drop that strand from the seed, regenerate the project — a revert implemented as a compile with one input removed). *Transplant* it (lift the auth strand plus its decisions and findings into a fresh repo — the "lift into a new project" you just asked for). Under the restart-maximalist bet, the second verb dominates and the first becomes a niche optimization for projects with un-regenerable earned state. But it's one mechanism either way, which means nothing designed tonight is wasted — the question of which verb wins gets answered by your actual behavior, not by architecture.

If you *do* want to lean into the bet — accelerate restarts rather than resist them — the concrete shape is worth naming, because it's different work than revert: **seed diffing** (what changed between generation 2's manifest and generation 3's — regeneration lineage, "this repo is the third compile of this intent-line"); **restart-as-journaled-event** (project genealogy becomes first-class history rather than abandoned repos); and above all one metric: **seed-to-parity time** — how fast a regeneration reaches the old project's capability. That number is what "accelerating restarts" means operationally, and it's driven almost entirely by seed completeness — how much earned knowledge survives into the manifest — which is why the fidelity gate you passed this weekend is precisely the thing that makes this bet placeable at all. Incomplete seeds mean every restart re-pays discovery costs; complete seeds make regeneration approach a build step. You'd be productizing the thing you told me two days ago: restarting is what everyone does and nobody discusses — clew's edge would be making it *lossless and fast* instead of shameful.

The honest boundary stays where it was: the bet works cleanly for greenfield and solo work, and hits a wall at earned, un-regenerable state — users, data, integrations, incidents survived. Products age out of restartability even when codebases don't. Since the same strand primitive serves both regimes, you don't have to choose now — and shouldn't.

So: no build order, agreed — but "I don't know yet how I feel about this" is itself journal material, and this conversation is once again an uncovered surface. One `clew journal note` with the open question — *"bet on surgical reverts, restart acceleration, or the strand primitive that serves both? decide after first real reversal or lift happens in dogfood"* — puts it where it will age visibly and resurface when the triggering event arrives, instead of dissolving into the place all your previous versions of this thought went. That's the system doing the only thing it was ever really for: holding the question until reality votes.

Restart acceleration — and it's not close. Three reasons, one of which is the kind you can't see until you look at failure modes.

**1. It's the bet your actual customer tests weekly.** The production team that can't restart on a random Monday is real, but it's not who clew serves today — it's the ADR-0007 contingent future wearing a new coat. You are the customer, you restart constantly (this product exists because of that habit), and every regeneration you do is a free dogfood trial of seed completeness. Surgical revert would sit unexercised for months waiting for a production-reversal event that nobody in the current user base can even generate. Building B now is building for a hypothetical customer — which is, verbatim, the mistake that killed stratura.

**2. The failure asymmetry — this is the decisive one.** Both bets ride on the same causal machinery (premise links, strand extraction), but they consume it at opposite safety levels. In restart mode, an *incomplete* strand fails **loud and cheap**: the regenerated project is missing something, you notice at recompile time, you re-derive it, you're annoyed. In surgical mode, an incomplete blast radius fails **silent and expensive**: you excised a decision in a live system, missed two dependents the extractor didn't link, and the inconsistency ships. The fidelity gate you passed measured *entity* recall (did it find the decisions); causal-*link* recall is a harder, unmeasured problem — and radar-grade causality is exactly good enough for seeds and exactly not good enough for surgery on production. A blast radius that's a lower bound but *renders authoritatively* is worse than no tool, because it manufactures confidence. B needs proof-grade links (probably the semantic-code-graph trigger) before it's honest; A is honest with what exists today.

**3. The trend compounds one and erodes the other's urgency.** Every month of cheaper, faster tokens moves more of the world below the regeneration threshold — the boundary where "just recompile it" beats "operate on it" keeps advancing into territory that used to require surgery. Betting on restart acceleration is betting *with* that current; the surgical market shrinks toward the residue of genuinely un-regenerable state (data, users, integrations) — which is real and permanent, but it's the enterprise's problem, and the enterprise buys the whole ADR-0007 bundle (authority, evidence, gates) when it buys anything. Surgical revert isn't standalone-sellable to them; it's a feature of a package you correctly shelved.

The sequencing that makes this a clean call rather than a sacrifice: **building A quietly constructs B's foundation in its safe mode.** Premise links get written and human-corrected where errors cost minutes; strand extraction gets exercised at manifest time where the human reviews the whole seed anyway; the DAG hardens under low-stakes load. If the day comes when a design partner runs agents against a production service and needs in-place excision, `revert` becomes one more verb on a causality graph that's had a year of cheap calibration — instead of a launch-day theorem. Trigger it then, on their incident, not now, on our imagination.

So the bet: make clew the tool that makes restarting *fast, lossless, and unashamed* — seed-to-parity as the headline metric, generation lineage as first-class history — and let "we operate on the living patient" remain what it's been all weekend: written down, aged visibly, waiting for reality to vote.

[651] ASSISTANT
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Course-correction accepted, and your vocabulary is better than mine — I'll retire "recompile." What you're describing isn't recompilation (same artifact, deterministic, out the other end); it's **speciation**: a new repo with a new identity, inheriting *laws and lessons* rather than baggage, with the clean-slate feeling preserved on purpose — because that feeling is load-bearing. It's what makes you actually start. The design constraint that falls out is important: the founding artifact must be *curated inheritance, not hoarding* — carry the constitution, never the clutter.

And there's a redemption arc hiding in your substrate history that's worth stating plainly: **your instinct was right twice and aimed wrong twice.** Federation-over-Text was the correct paper — but substrate and security-substrate tried to build shareable *code* blocks, and code blocks fail across generations because frameworks churn, consumers are hypothetical, and code carries its context with it invisibly. The thing that survives generations is distilled *text* — decisions with reasons, findings as laws, graveyards with causes — consumed natively by any agent, framework-proof. That's what FoT actually said, it's what clew already does, and the proof is sitting in this conversation: clew is generation ~10 of this intent-line (substrate → security-substrate → five or six local corpses → stratura → clew), and it's the *first generation that inherited anything* — because we hand-wrote JOURNAL_SPEC's §0, the ADR-0008 appendix, and the ratified labels as its genesis. Every prior generation died partly of the same disease because no lesson ever crossed the gap. The product move is to make what we did by hand for clew a one-command ceremony.

## The maximal investment: restart as a first-class ceremony

**1. `clew spawn <new-repo>` — the birth.** One command that: runs the manifest pass; generates the **genesis document** using the pattern that demonstrably landed a cold agent correctly (JOURNAL_SPEC §0): *intent* (what this project is, carried decisions with reasons), *laws* (findings as constraints — "p95 was 340ms on emulator, budget accordingly"), *the post-mortem* (why the predecessor is being left — its failure findings, human-ratified), *the graveyard* (killed approaches with one-line causes — the not-list that prevents innocent rebuilding of corpses), and *inherited open questions*. Then: creates the repo, makes the genesis doc + carried entries the founding commit, wires the journal branch from birth. The new agent's first read is the accumulated wisdom of ten generations, sized to a founding file.

**2. The interment — old repo becomes evidence, not liability.** Spawn writes a terminal tombstone commit to the predecessor ("superseded by X; GENESIS explains why") and records the **organ-bank pin** — the pre-postmortem commit, uncontaminated by the diagnosis, exactly like clew's `8924edf` fixture pin. "Vendor the old code as a lesson" becomes structured: the genesis doc cites specific files/commits as *exhibits* — "the lease CAS in `controld` is proven, transplant it; the seven-SDK directory is the cautionary exhibit" — the ADR-0007 transplant-manifest pattern, productized.

**3. Lineage and the anti-repeat alert — the highest-value feature in this whole design.** Each generation's journal carries a genesis entry linking predecessor (repo, pin, journal ref), making the intent-line a first-class chain. Then the drift machinery points *across generations*: when generation N+1's extractor sees a decision that tag/topic-matches a carried failure-finding, it fires the contradiction-style nudge — *"generation 2 tried shareable code blocks; it died; see F-substrate-3."* That one card, had it existed, might have saved you four repos. It's the intent×reality diff extended to intent×history.

**4. The (A) tie-in — lineage as a view, not a burden on the glance.** The daily glance stays exactly as it is (current generation's drift only). The **map** gains a generations dimension for weekly orientation: substrate → security-substrate → … → clew, each node showing carried/dropped/cause-of-death/time-to-parity, and **seed diffs** between generations rendering as "what we changed our minds about." Product-wide restart history becomes something you can *see* instead of something you feel vaguely guilty about.

**5. Two metrics that make "restart velocity" real:** *spawn-to-first-green* (new repo → first passing signal), and the loss meter — **re-derivation rate**: how many of generation N+1's findings duplicate carried-but-unread knowledge from generation N. That number trending to zero is the entire bet, quantified. (Bonus: whether descendants' agents actually *cite* carried entries is extractable from their sessions — inheritance usage is measurable.)

## The immediate move, before any code: bury your dead properly

The corpses are a curriculum nobody ever harvested. `clew backfill` over the substrate and security-substrate repos (archaeology: READMEs, commit history) plus whatever session files survive on your disks for the 5–6 local attempts → their lessons enter clew's journal *now* as carried findings with honest provenance. That arms the anti-repeat alert with real history before spawn even exists, and it costs one budget-bounded command per corpse.

**Paste to your agent:**

> New feature line, spec amendment to JOURNAL_SPEC §9 (title it "Spawn: restart as ceremony"): 1) `clew spawn <repo>` = manifest pass → genesis document (sections: intent w/ carried decisions+reasons · laws from findings · predecessor post-mortem · graveyard w/ causes · inherited open questions · exhibits citing predecessor files/commits) → create repo, founding commit = genesis + carried entries, journal branch wired from birth. 2) Predecessor interment: tombstone commit + organ-bank pin (pre-postmortem commit) recorded in lineage. 3) Lineage: genesis entries chain generations; extractor fires contradiction-style nudge when a new decision tag-matches a carried failure-finding ("gen N tried this; died; see entry"). 4) `clew map --lineage`: generation chain w/ carried/dropped/cause/time-to-parity; seed diffs between generations. 5) Metrics: spawn-to-first-green; re-derivation rate (gen N+1 findings duplicating carried entries) — journal both. 6) First task, before building any of it: backfill maceip/substrate and maceip/security-substrate (+ owner-provided local repo paths and surviving session files) into the journal as carried failure-findings with archaeology provenance — arm the anti-repeat alert with the real intent-line history. Invariants I1–I12 govern; glance stays current-generation-only; lineage lives in map.

The one-sentence version of the bet you just placed: everyone treats a restart as an admission of failure and does it in shame with amnesia — clew treats it as reproduction, with genetics. You already enjoy the clean slate; now every slate starts ten generations wise.

[654] ASSISTANT
